● My plan is ready. Here's the summary before I start building.

  What I'll produce: A single Markdown dossier at
  nsut-innovators-court/research-dossier.md — the complete research knowledge
  base behind the (already-existing) slide template, not slides themselves.
  Target 20,000+ words across all 22 required sections plus an Executive
  Summary.

  How I'll build it (per your choices):
  - One giant file, filled section-by-section across multiple turns (tracked
  with a todo list so nothing drops).
  - Blended research: WebSearch/WebFetch to verify load-bearing numbers (market
  sizing/CAGR, funding rounds, regulation dates, adoption stats) plus heavy live
  research throughout — with a citation legend distinguishing [SRC: …]
  (sourced/verified), [EST] (modeled estimate), and [APPROX] (recalled,
  unverified).
  - Markdown only — Mermaid diagrams (problem tree, fishbone, 5-Whys,
  architecture, sequence, ERD, mind map), comparison matrices, and tables
  throughout.

  Depth targets baked in: ≥30 case studies, ≥50 competitors, 100+ clustered
  papers, 30 ranked solution ideas → one chosen, 100+ verified statistics, full
  financial/architecture/roadmap/risk analysis, and a categorized bibliography.

  The plan is written to the plan file. Approving it will take me out of plan
  mode and I'll begin the first pass (file skeleton + citation legend +
  Executive Summary + Section 1), doing live verification as I go.

          +ount *emerging PETs*. Confidential-computing forecasts are the
          + wildest because some analysts count only software, others cou
          +nt the entire hardware TCO of confidential-capable servers — h
          +ence the 45–90% CAGR spread. **For our TAM we anchor on the PE
          +Ts category (M1) plus the confidential-collaboration subset of
          + M2/M3, deliberately excluding pure compliance-form software w
          +e would not build.**                                          
      292 +

          +ion slice of privacy software (not the whole US$30 B, only the
          + ~30–40% that is genuinely data-sharing/collaboration rather t
          +han DSAR paperwork) yields ~US$12–15 B. `[EST — derived from M
          +1 + a 30–40% subset of M2]`

      299 +- Focus verticals where **cross-institution data collaboration

      300 +- These four verticals represent an estimated **~55–65% of PET
          + demand** (regulated data is where PETs are non-optional). `[E
          +ST]`                                                          
      301 +- **SAM (2030): ~US$3–4 B.** `[EST = ~25–30% of TAM, reflectin
          +g our vertical + geographic focus]`                           
      302 +

          +ssive under-served regulated data (health, credit), and (d) a

          +e best *brand/credibility* market; the US is the largest *reve
          +nue* market.

      361 +                                                              
      362 +# 3. TECHNOLOGY LANDSCAPE                                     
      363 +                                                              
      364 +> This section is the technical heart. Each technology is expl
          +ained (how it works), then rated on **maturity, performance co
          +st, scalability, adoption, and limitations**, and finally plac
          +ed in a **comparison matrix** and a **defense-in-depth stack**
          +. The key meta-insight: **no single PET solves the problem; th
          +e winning system composes several.**                          
      365 +                                                              
      366 +## 3.1 The master comparison matrix (read this first)         
      367 +                                                              
      368 +| Technology | What it protects | How it works (1 line) | Perf
          + overhead | Maturity | Best for | Key limitation |            
      369 +|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|                                 
      370 +| **Federated Learning (FL)** | Raw data never leaves silo | T
          +rain local, share model updates | Low–moderate (comms) | Produ
          +ction (Google, 2017) | Multi-silo model training | Updates can
          + leak → needs DP/TEE |                                        
      371 +| **Split Learning** | Raw data + full model | Split NN; each 
          +party holds layers | Moderate | Research→pilot | Vertically-pa
          +rtitioned data | Smashed-data leakage risk |                  
      372 +| **Differential Privacy (DP)** | Output leakage about individ
          +uals | Add calibrated noise; bound ε | Accuracy loss | Product
          +ion (Census, Apple) | Statistics, DP-SGD training | Utility–pr
          +ivacy tradeoff; ε hard to set |                               
      373 +| **Secure Multi-Party Computation (SMPC)** | Inputs of each p
          +arty | Jointly compute f() revealing only output | High (comms
          +/rounds) | Pilot→production (niche) | Joint analytics, private
          + set intersection | Communication-heavy at scale |            
      374 +| **Homomorphic Encryption (HE)** | Data in use | Compute dire
          +ctly on ciphertext | Very high (100–10,000×) | Emerging (parti
          +al in prod) | Outsourced compute on encrypted data | Slow; lim
          +ited ops (FHE) |                                              
      375 +| **Trusted Execution Environments (TEE)** | Data in use (from
          + host/cloud) | Hardware enclave + remote attestation | Low (5–
          +30%) | Production (SGX/SEV/TDX/H100) | Confidential AI, secure
          + enclaves | Trusts hardware vendor; side-channels |           
      376 +| **Zero-Knowledge Proofs (ZKP)** | Prove without revealing | 
          +Prove statement true w/o data | Moderate–high | Production (bl
          +ockchain), emerging elsewhere | Verifiable claims, private aut
          +h | Proving cost; circuit complexity |                        
      377 +| **Confidential Computing** | Data + code in use | TEE + atte
          +station as a platform | Low | Production (all 3 clouds) | Whol
          +e-workload protection | Same as TEE |                         
      378 +| **Synthetic Data** | The real records | Generate artificial 
          +data w/ real stats | Training cost | Emerging→production | Sha
          +ring, testing, augmentation | May leak/underrepresent; fidelit
          +y vs privacy |                                                
      379 +| **Data Clean Rooms** | Cross-party raw data | Governed share
          +d environment, no raw export | Low | Production (Snowflake, Go
          +ogle) | Adtech/measurement collab | Trust in room operator; no
          +t individual-facing |                                         
      380 +| **DID / SSI / Verifiable Credentials** | Identity + consent 
          +control | User holds cryptographic credentials/DIDs | Low | Em
          +erging (W3C standard) | Individual control, portable consent |
          + Ecosystem/adoption immaturity |                              
      381 +| **Tokenization** | Sensitive fields | Replace value with non
          +-sensitive token | Very low | Production (payments) | PCI, fie
          +ld-level protection | Not "compute on data"; vaulting |       
      382 +                                                              
      383 +## 3.2 Federated Learning (FL)                                
      384 +                                                              
      385 +**How it works.** Instead of copying data to a central server,
          + a shared model is sent to each data holder (device or institu
          +tion). Each trains locally on its own data and returns only **
          +model updates** (gradients/weights). A coordinator aggregates 
          +updates (e.g., **Federated Averaging**, McMahan et al. 2017) i
          +nto an improved global model. Raw data never moves. `[SRC: McM
          +ahan et al., AISTATS 2017]`                                   
      386 +                                                              
      387 +- **Cross-device FL:** millions of phones (Google Gboard next-
          +word prediction, since 2017). `[SRC: Google AI, 2017]`        
      388 +- **Cross-silo FL:** a handful of institutions (e.g., hospital
          +s, banks) — the enterprise-relevant flavor.                   
      389 +                                                              
      390 +**Advantages.** Data stays local (regulatory + security win); 
          +leverages edge compute; enables collaboration among competitor
          +s/regulated parties.                                          
      391 +**Disadvantages.** Model updates *can leak* training data (gra
          +dient-inversion attacks) → must combine with DP and/or TEE; no
          +n-IID data across silos hurts convergence; communication overh
          +ead; systems-heterogeneity (stragglers).                      
      392 +**Scalability.** Cross-device: proven to billions. Cross-silo:
          + proven to dozens of institutions (NVIDIA FLARE, Owkin).      
      393 +**Cost.** Communication + coordination infra; lower than centr
          +alizing petabytes.                                            
      394 +**Adoption.** Google, Apple, NVIDIA (Clara/FLARE), Owkin (drug
          + discovery), Intel (OpenFL), WeBank (FATE). Production-grade. 
      395 +**Limitations.** Not private *by itself* — FL is a data-minimi
          +zation pattern, not a privacy guarantee. This is a crucial nua
          +nce judges love: **FL + DP + TEE** is the real recipe.        
      396 +                                                              
      397 +## 3.3 Split Learning                                         
      398 +                                                              
      399 +**How it works.** A neural network is split across parties: th
          +e data holder computes forward pass up to a "cut layer" and se
          +nds only the intermediate activations ("smashed data") to the 
          +server, which completes the network. Gradients flow back. Neit
          +her side sees the other's full data/model. `[SRC: Gupta & Rask
          +ar, MIT, 2018]`                                               
      400 +**Advantages.** Lower client compute than full FL; good for ve
          +rtically-partitioned data; model can stay partly secret.      
      401 +**Disadvantages.** Smashed data can leak; sequential training 
          +across many clients is slow; less mature than FL.             
      402 +**Best for.** Settings where clients are weak (IoT) or data is
          + vertically partitioned (different features held by different 
          +parties).                                                     
      403 +                                                              
      404 +## 3.4 Differential Privacy (DP)                              
      405 +                                                              
      406 +**How it works.** Add **calibrated random noise** so that the 
          +presence/absence of any single individual changes the output b
          +y a bounded amount. The **privacy budget ε (epsilon)** quantif
          +ies leakage: smaller ε = more privacy, less accuracy. Formaliz
          +ed by Dwork et al. (2006). `[SRC: Dwork, McSherry, Nissim, Smi
          +th, TCC 2006]`                                                
      407 +- **Local DP:** noise added on-device before data leaves (Appl
          +e, Google RAPPOR) — strong privacy, more noise.               
      408 +- **Central DP:** trusted curator adds noise to aggregate outp
          +uts (US Census) — better utility, needs trusted curator (or a 
          +TEE to remove that trust).                                    
      409 +- **DP-SGD:** DP applied to model training (clip gradients + a
          +dd noise) — the standard for private ML. `[SRC: Abadi et al., 
          +"Deep Learning with Differential Privacy," CCS 2016]`         
      410 +                                                              
      411 +**Advantages.** *Mathematical, composable, future-proof* guara
          +ntee — the only PET that quantifies leakage. Provably resistan
          +t to re-identification & membership inference.                
      412 +**Disadvantages.** Utility loss (the central tradeoff); choosi
          +ng ε is a policy nightmare (US Census faced litigation over ut
          +ility); not intuitive to non-experts.                         
      413 +**Adoption.** US Census 2020 (national scale), Apple (emoji/Qu
          +ickType telemetry), Google (Chrome, RAPPOR), Microsoft, Linked
          +In, Uber. Production. `[SRC: US Census Bureau, 2020]`         
      414 +**Limitation.** Protects *outputs*, not data-in-use — combine 
          +with FL/TEE for end-to-end.                                   
      415 +                                                              
      416 +## 3.5 Secure Multi-Party Computation (SMPC / MPC)            
      417 +                                                              
      418 +**How it works.** Multiple parties jointly compute a function 
          +over their private inputs such that **no party learns anything
          + except the output**. Built on secret sharing and/or garbled c
          +ircuits (Yao, 1982). Classic use: **Private Set Intersection**
          + (find common customers without revealing customer lists). `[S
          +RC: Yao, FOCS 1982]`                                          
      419 +**Advantages.** Strong cryptographic guarantee; no trusted thi
          +rd party; exact (no accuracy loss).                           
      420 +**Disadvantages.** Communication/round complexity → slow for l
          +arge/deep computations; hard to program.                      
      421 +**Adoption.** Boston wage-gap study (MPC to compute pay statis
          +tics across employers); Google/Meta private set intersection f
          +or ad measurement; Partisia, Sharemind, Inpher. Production in 
          +niches.                                                       
      422 +**Best for.** Joint statistics, private matching, threshold cr
          +yptography.                                                   
      423 +                                                              
      424 +## 3.6 Homomorphic Encryption (HE)                            
      425 +                                                              
      426 +**How it works.** Perform computation directly on **encrypted 
          +data**; decrypt to get the result as if computed on plaintext.
          + **Partially HE** (one operation type) is fast and old (RSA is
          + multiplicatively homomorphic). **Fully HE (FHE)** (arbitrary 
          +computation) was proven possible by Gentry (2009). `[SRC: Gent
          +ry, STOC 2009]`                                               
      427 +**Advantages.** The "holy grail": outsource computation to an 
          +untrusted cloud with zero exposure; no trusted hardware needed
          +.                                                             
      428 +**Disadvantages.** **Extremely slow** — historically 10³–10⁶× 
          +overhead; ciphertext expansion; limited practical operations. 
          +Improving fast (Microsoft SEAL, OpenFHE, Zama's fhEVM/Concrete
          +) but still not general-purpose for deep learning at scale.   
      429 +**Adoption.** Emerging: private inference (Zama), encrypted se
          +arch, some fintech. Not yet mainstream for large-model trainin
          +g.                                                            
      430 +**Best for.** Small, well-defined computations on highly sensi
          +tive data where hardware trust is unacceptable.               
      431 +                                                              
      432 +## 3.7 Trusted Execution Environments (TEE) & Confidential Com
          +puting                                                        
      433 +                                                              
      434 +**How it works.** A hardware-isolated **enclave** runs code an
          +d data such that even the OS, hypervisor, or cloud operator ca
          +nnot read them. **Remote attestation** cryptographically prove
          +s to a remote party *which code* is running inside a genuine e
          +nclave before secrets are released.                           
      435 +- **Intel SGX** (process-level enclaves), **Intel TDX** & **AM
          +D SEV-SNP** (VM-level), **ARM CCA**, **NVIDIA H100/H200 Confid
          +ential Computing** (GPU enclaves for AI). `[SRC: Confidential 
          +Computing Consortium]`                                        
      436 +**Advantages.** Low overhead (single-digit to ~30%); protects 
          +**data-in-use** (the gap DP/encryption-at-rest leave); enables
          + running *any* workload confidentially; attestation gives veri
          +fiable trust.                                                 
      437 +**Disadvantages.** **Trusts the hardware vendor** and its firm
          +ware; historical **side-channel attacks** (Spectre/Foreshadow/
          +SGAxe); enclave memory limits (older SGX); vendor lock-in.    
      438 +**Adoption.** GA on **Azure, AWS (Nitro Enclaves), GCP**; NVID
          +IA confidential GPUs for AI. This is the **fastest-maturing PE
          +T** and the backbone of "Confidential AI."                    
      439 +**Best for.** Running FL aggregation, DP curation, or model tr
          +aining/inference where you must not trust the host — the "trus
          +ted glue" that makes other PETs practical.                    
      440 +                                                              
      441 +## 3.8 Zero-Knowledge Proofs (ZKP)                            
      442 +                                                              
      443 +**How it works.** A prover convinces a verifier that a stateme
          +nt is true **without revealing why** (no underlying data). Mod
          +ern succinct forms: **zk-SNARKs, zk-STARKs**. Originating from
          + Goldwasser–Micali–Rackoff (1985). `[SRC: GMR, 1985]`         
      444 +**Advantages.** Prove facts about data (age > 18, income < thr
          +eshold, "this credential is valid") without revealing the data
          +; verifiable computation; strong for **individual-controlled s
          +elective disclosure**.                                        
      445 +**Disadvantages.** Proof-generation cost; circuit-writing comp
          +lexity; relatively new outside blockchain.                    
      446 +**Adoption.** Massive in blockchain/privacy coins & zk-rollups
          + (zkSync, StarkNet); emerging in identity (Polygon ID, zk-base
          +d KYC), Cloudflare privacy pass.                              
      447 +**Best for.** The **consent/identity layer** — proving eligibi
          +lity & consent without exposing personal attributes.          
      448 +                                                              
      449 +## 3.9 Blockchain / DLT                                       
      450 +                                                              
      451 +**How it works.** Append-only, tamper-evident distributed ledg
          +er; here used **not** for storing personal data (a common anti
          +-pattern — immutable + personal data conflicts with GDPR erasu
          +re) but for **consent receipts, audit trails, and access-grant
          + records**.                                                   
      452 +**Advantages.** Tamper-evident audit; decentralized trust; goo
          +d for a **verifiable consent/audit ledger**.                  
      453 +**Disadvantages.** Do **not** put personal data on-chain (immu
          +tability vs right-to-erasure); throughput/cost; often over-eng
          +ineered vs a signed append-only log.                          
      454 +**Adoption.** Estonia's KSI blockchain for government-record i
          +ntegrity; supply-chain provenance; SSI anchoring.             
      455 +**Best for.** Anchoring consent/audit metadata (hashes, not da
          +ta) for tamper-evidence.                                      
      456 +                                                              
      457 +## 3.10 DID / SSI / Verifiable Credentials (the individual-con
          +trol layer)                                                   
      458 +                                                              
      459 +**How it works.**                                             
      460 +- **Decentralized Identifiers (DIDs)** — W3C standard; identif
          +iers the user controls, not issued by a platform.             
      461 +- **Self-Sovereign Identity (SSI)** — the user holds their ide
          +ntity/credentials in a wallet, presents them selectively.     
      462 +- **Verifiable Credentials (VCs)** — W3C standard; cryptograph
          +ically signed claims (a hospital issues "patient X consented t
          +o use Y") the user stores and presents; verifiable without cal
          +ling the issuer. `[SRC: W3C VC Data Model / DID Core]`        
      463 +**Advantages.** This is the **technical embodiment of "individ
          +uals retain control"** — portable, revocable, selective-disclo
          +sure consent that the individual holds. Pairs with ZKP for pri
          +vacy-preserving proofs.                                       
      464 +**Disadvantages.** Ecosystem immaturity; key-management UX (lo
          +st keys = lost identity); interoperability still settling; ado
          +ption chicken-and-egg.                                        
      465 +**Adoption.** EU Digital Identity Wallet (eIDAS 2.0), India Di
          +giLocker (adjacent), Microsoft Entra Verified ID, Sovrin/Hyper
          +ledger Indy, Polygon ID. Emerging but standards-backed.       
      466 +**Best for.** The consent, identity, and audit control plane o
          +f our solution.                                               
      467 +                                                              
      468 +## 3.11 Synthetic Data                                        
      469 +                                                              
      470 +**How it works.** Train a generative model (GAN, VAE, diffusio
          +n, or LLM) on real data to produce **artificial records** that
          + preserve statistical properties but correspond to no real ind
          +ividual. Often combined with DP (DP-synthetic data) for formal
          + guarantees.                                                  
      471 +**Advantages.** Shareable, testable, augments scarce data (rar
          +e diseases); sidesteps some consent issues; good for dev/test.
      472 +**Disadvantages.** Can **leak/memorize** real records if not D
          +P-trained; fidelity–privacy tradeoff; may embed/amplify bias; 
          +regulators wary of "is this really anonymous?".               
      473 +**Adoption.** MOSTLY AI, Gretel, Hazy, Syntegra, NVIDIA; used 
          +in finance, health, autonomous-vehicle training.              
      474 +**Best for.** Data sharing/testing where approximate distribut
          +ions suffice; augmentation.                                   
      475 +                                                              
      476 +## 3.12 Data Clean Rooms                                      
      477 +                                                              
      478 +**How it works.** A governed, neutral environment where multip
          +le parties bring data, run *approved* queries/joins, and expor
          +t only aggregate results — **raw data never leaves**. Increasi
          +ngly PET-enhanced (DP on outputs, TEEs).                      
      479 +**Advantages.** Practical, adopted, revenue-generating (adtech
          + post-cookie); familiar to enterprises.                       
      480 +**Disadvantages.** Trust in the room operator; primarily **B2B
          +, not individual-facing** (the individual has no seat); can be
          + circumvented by careful querying without DP.                 
      481 +**Adoption.** Google Ads Data Hub, Amazon Marketing Cloud, Sno
          +wflake (acquired **Samooha**, 2023), Habu (acquired by LiveRam
          +p), InfoSum, AWS Clean Rooms. Production, growing fast.       
      482 +**Gap we exploit:** clean rooms serve *institutions*; **the in
          +dividual is absent**. Our differentiator is putting the indivi
          +dual's consent/control into the collaboration.                
      483 +                                                              
      484 +## 3.13 Supporting primitives                                 
      485 +                                                              
      486 +- **Secure APIs / consent APIs** — India's **Account Aggregato
          +r** consent-artifact API is the reference: machine-readable, g
          +ranular, time-bound, revocable consent flowing between financi
          +al institutions. `[SRC: Sahamati / RBI AA framework]`         
      487 +- **Tokenization** — replace sensitive values with tokens (pay
          +ments/PCI); complements but is not "compute on data."         
      488 +- **Encryption standards** — AES-256 (at rest), TLS 1.3 (in tr
          +ansit); the *baseline*, not the innovation. The frontier is **
          +data-in-use** (TEE/HE).                                       
      489 +- **Identity/auth standards** — OAuth 2.0 / OIDC (delegated au
          +th), FIDO2/WebAuthn (passwordless), SAML (enterprise), plus th
          +e DID/VC stack above.                                         
      490 +                                                              
      491 +## 3.14 The defense-in-depth composition (the key architectura
          +l claim)                                                      
      492 +                                                              
      493 +```mermaid                                                    
      494 +graph TB                                                      
      495 +    subgraph "Control Plane (individual retains control)"     
      496 +    ID[DID / SSI Wallet]                                      
      497 +    VC[Verifiable Credentials = consent]                      
      498 +    ZK[ZKP selective disclosure]                              
      499 +    LEDGER[Tamper-evident consent+audit ledger]               
      500 +    end                                                       
      501 +    subgraph "Compute Plane (compute goes to data)"           
      502 +    FL[Federated Learning across silos]                       
      503 +    TEE[TEE / Confidential Computing<br/>hosts aggregation & t
          +raining]                                                      
      504 +    DP[Differential Privacy on outputs/gradients]             
      505 +    MPC[SMPC / PSI for joint matching]                        
      506 +    end                                                       
      507 +    subgraph "Data Plane (data never centralized)"            
      508 +    S1[Silo A raw data]                                       
      509 +    S2[Silo B raw data]                                       
      510 +    S3[Silo C raw data]                                       
      511 +    end                                                       
      512 +    ID --> VC --> LEDGER                                      
      513 +    VC -. gates .-> FL                                        
      514 +    ZK --> VC                                                 
      515 +    S1 --> FL                                                 
      516 +    S2 --> FL                                                 
      517 +    S3 --> FL                                                 
      518 +    FL --> TEE                                                
      519 +    TEE --> DP                                                
      520 +    MPC -. optional joins .-> TEE                             
      521 +    DP --> OUT[Model / insight output]                        
      522 +    LEDGER -. audits .-> OUT                                  
      523 +```                                                           
      524 +                                                              
      525 +**The thesis restated technically:** *FL* keeps data local, *T
          +EE* makes the aggregation/training trustworthy even on untrust
          +ed infrastructure, *DP* bounds output leakage, *SMPC* handles 
          +private joins, and *SSI/VC/ZKP + an audit ledger* give the **i
          +ndividual** a revocable, verifiable grant over the whole proce
          +ss. No single technology is novel; **the composition, the UX, 
          +and the individual-facing control are.**                      
      526 +                                                              
      527 +## 3.15 Maturity & cost snapshot (for feasibility slides)     
      528 +                                                              
      529 +| Tech | TRL (1–9) | Relative cost to deploy | Verdict for our
          + stack |                                                      
      530 +|---|---|---|---|                                             
      531 +| FL | 8–9 | Low–med | **Core** |                             
      532 +| TEE / Confidential Computing | 8–9 | Low (cloud GA) | **Core
          + (trust glue)** |                                             
      533 +| DP | 8–9 | Low (algorithmic) | **Core (output bound)** |    
      534 +| SMPC / PSI | 6–7 | Med–high | **Selective (private joins)** 
          +|                                                             
      535 +| ZKP | 7–8 | Med | **Control layer** |                       
      536 +| SSI/DID/VC | 6–7 | Med | **Control layer (differentiator)** 
          +|                                                             
      537 +| HE / FHE | 4–6 | High | **Watch; niche only** |             
      538 +| Synthetic data | 6–8 | Med | **Adjacent feature** |         
      539 +| Clean rooms | 8–9 | Low–med | **Competitor pattern to beat**
          + |

      543 +# 4. GLOBAL CASE STUDIES (≥30)                                
      544 +                                                              
      545 +> Format per case: **Problem → Solution → Technology → Outcome
          + → Lesson.** These are the evidence base that "this works in p
          +roduction," organized from Big Tech → healthcare → finance → p
          +ublic digital infrastructure. Lessons are written to be direct
          +ly quotable on Feasibility/Scalability slides.                
      546 +                                                              
      547 +## A. Big Tech PET deployments                                
      548 +                                                              
      549 +**1. Google — Gboard Federated Learning (2017→)**             
      550 +- *Problem:* improve next-word prediction without uploading us
          +ers' keystrokes.                                              
      551 +- *Solution:* on-device federated learning across billions of 
          +Android phones.                                               
      552 +- *Technology:* Federated Averaging + secure aggregation + (la
          +ter) DP.                                                      
      553 +- *Outcome:* production model improvement with raw text never 
          +leaving the device; foundational proof FL works at planetary s
          +cale. `[SRC: Google AI blog, 2017]`                           
      554 +- *Lesson:* FL is production-real at billions-of-devices scale
          + — feasibility is not the question, usability/composition is. 
      555 +                                                              
      556 +**2. Apple — Differential Privacy telemetry (2016→)**         
      557 +- *Problem:* learn usage patterns (emoji, QuickType, Safari) w
          +ithout profiling individuals.                                 
      558 +- *Solution:* **local** differential privacy — noise added on-
          +device before data leaves.                                    
      559 +- *Technology:* local DP, count-mean-sketch.                  
      560 +- *Outcome:* privacy became Apple's marketing spine ("Privacy.
          + That's iPhone."); DP normalized for consumers. `[SRC: Apple D
          +ifferential Privacy Overview]`                                
      561 +- *Lesson:* privacy as **brand differentiator** — proof the ma
          +rket rewards it.                                              
      562 +                                                              
      563 +**3. Apple — App Tracking Transparency (2021)**               
      564 +- *Problem:* cross-app tracking without meaningful consent.   
      565 +- *Solution:* mandatory per-app consent prompt for tracking.  
      566 +- *Outcome:* ~US$10 B+ hit to adtech (Meta cited ~US$10 B 2022
          + revenue impact) `[APPROX]`; showed **consent controls have ma
          +ssive economic power**.                                       
      567 +- *Lesson:* individual control, when enforced at the platform 
          +layer, reshapes entire industries.                            
      568 +                                                              
      569 +**4. Microsoft — Confidential Computing (Azure) + SEAL**      
      570 +- *Problem:* enterprises won't move sensitive workloads to clo
          +ud they don't fully trust.                                    
      571 +- *Solution:* Azure Confidential VMs/containers (SGX, SEV-SNP,
          + TDX) + open-source **Microsoft SEAL** (HE library).          
      572 +- *Technology:* TEE + attestation; HE library.                
      573 +- *Outcome:* confidential computing GA; a reference platform f
          +or "untrusted cloud" workloads.                               
      574 +- *Lesson:* the big clouds have already built the compute plan
          +e — a startup should orchestrate on top, not rebuild it.      
      575 +                                                              
      576 +**5. NVIDIA — Clara / FLARE + Confidential GPUs**             
      577 +- *Problem:* hospitals can't pool medical images to train bett
          +er models.                                                    
      578 +- *Solution:* **NVIDIA FLARE** (federated learning framework) 
          ++ H100 confidential-computing GPUs.                           
      579 +- *Technology:* cross-silo FL + GPU TEE.                      
      580 +- *Outcome:* enabled multi-hospital studies (see EXAM below). 
      581 +- *Lesson:* hardware + framework maturity means confidential A
          +I is procurable today.                                        
      582 +                                                              
      583 +**6. NVIDIA + 20 hospitals — "EXAM" COVID model (2020–21)**   
      584 +- *Problem:* predict COVID patients' oxygen needs; no single h
          +ospital had enough diverse data.                              
      585 +- *Solution:* federated learning across **20 hospitals on 4 co
          +ntinents**, no data sharing.                                  
      586 +- *Technology:* NVIDIA FLARE federated learning.              
      587 +- *Outcome:* published in *Nature Medicine* (2021); the federa
          +ted model generalized better than single-site models, improvin
          +g prediction. `[SRC: Dayan et al., Nature Medicine, 2021]`    
      588 +- *Lesson:* **the killer proof** — multi-institution privacy-p
          +reserving AI *outperforms* siloed AI on a life-and-death task.
      589 +                                                              
      590 +**7. Google — Private Set Intersection / Password Checkup**   
      591 +- *Problem:* warn users if their password appears in a breach 
          +without revealing their password or the breach DB.            
      592 +- *Solution:* cryptographic PSI.                              
      593 +- *Technology:* SMPC/PSI + oblivious pseudorandom functions.  
      594 +- *Outcome:* shipped in Chrome to hundreds of millions.       
      595 +- *Lesson:* SMPC is production-real for well-scoped problems. 
      596 +                                                              
      597 +**8. Meta — Private Lift / on-device & PET ad measurement**   
      598 +- *Problem:* measure ad effectiveness post-ATT without individ
          +ual tracking.                                                 
      599 +- *Solution:* MPC-based "Private Lift" measurement; investment
          + in on-device aggregation.                                    
      600 +- *Technology:* SMPC.                                         
      601 +- *Outcome:* PET-based measurement as the post-cookie path.   
      602 +- *Lesson:* even the largest data extractor is forced toward P
          +ETs by regulation + platform changes.                         
      603 +                                                              
      604 +**9. OpenAI / Anthropic — data-use & privacy tensions (2023→)*
          +*                                                             
      605 +- *Problem:* LLMs trained on scraped data raise consent/copyri
          +ght/privacy suits (NYT v. OpenAI; training-data extraction).  
      606 +- *Solution (emerging):* opt-outs, enterprise "no-training" da
          +ta commitments, provenance efforts.                           
      607 +- *Technology:* policy + (nascent) machine unlearning, data-pr
          +ovenance.                                                     
      608 +- *Outcome:* live litigation; unresolved — illustrates the *un
          +solved* frontier.                                             
      609 +- *Lesson:* generative AI made "whose data trained this?" a bo
          +ard-level and courtroom issue → tailwind for provenance/consen
          +t tooling.                                                    
      610 +                                                              
      611 +**10. Microsoft — Entra Verified ID (SSI)**                   
      612 +- *Problem:* portable, user-held verifiable identity/credentia
          +ls.                                                           
      613 +- *Solution:* W3C VC-based verifiable credentials in the Entra
          + identity platform.                                           
      614 +- *Technology:* DIDs + VCs.                                   
      615 +- *Outcome:* enterprise SSI going mainstream.                 
      616 +- *Lesson:* the individual-control layer has big-vendor backin
          +g → standards are safe to build on.                           
      617 +                                                              
      618 +## B. Healthcare & life sciences                              
      619 +                                                              
      620 +**11. Owkin — federated drug discovery**                      
      621 +- *Problem:* pharma & hospitals can't pool patient data for on
          +cology research.                                              
      622 +- *Solution:* federated learning across hospital/pharma data; 
          +MELLODDY consortium.                                          
      623 +- *Technology:* cross-silo FL (Substra open-source).          
      624 +- *Outcome:* raised **~US$334 M** total; **US$180 M** strategi
          +c equity from **Sanofi (2021)**; evolved into a ~billion-dolla
          +r precision-medicine platform. `[SRC: Sanofi PR 2021; PitchBoo
          +k/Caplight, 2024–26]`                                         
      625 +- *Lesson:* federated data collaboration is **venture-fundable
          + and pharma-validated** at scale.                             
      626 +                                                              
      627 +**12. MELLODDY consortium (2019–2022)**                       
      628 +- *Problem:* 10 pharma competitors each hold chemical/bioactiv
          +ity data; pooling is impossible (IP + secrecy).               
      629 +- *Solution:* federated learning so rival pharmas jointly trai
          +n a drug-discovery model **without revealing their compounds**
          +.                                                             
      630 +- *Technology:* FL + secure aggregation (Owkin/Substra).      
      631 +- *Outcome:* proved competitors can co-train models; improved 
          +predictive performance. `[SRC: MELLODDY project / IMI]`       
      632 +- *Lesson:* **competitors will collaborate if privacy is crypt
          +ographically guaranteed** — the exact value proposition.      
      633 +                                                              
      634 +**13. Mayo Clinic — Platform + de-identified data collaboratio
          +n**                                                           
      635 +- *Problem:* monetize/enable research on clinical data while p
          +rotecting patients.                                           
      636 +- *Solution:* Mayo Clinic Platform with de-identification + go
          +verned distributed access; partnerships (nference, Google).   
      637 +- *Technology:* de-identification, secure enclaves, federated 
          +access.                                                       
      638 +- *Outcome:* a leading model for compliant clinical-data colla
          +boration.                                                     
      639 +- *Lesson:* trusted institutions can become data-collaboration
          + platforms with the right governance.                         
      640 +                                                              
      641 +**14. UK NHS — Care.data failure (2013–2016)**                
      642 +- *Problem:* pool GP records for research/planning.           
      643 +- *Solution (failed):* centralize records with weak consent/co
          +mmunication.                                                  
      644 +- *Outcome:* **scrapped in 2016** after public backlash over c
          +onsent and commercial data-sharing fears. `[SRC: NHS/UK gov, 2
          +016]`                                                         
      645 +- *Lesson:* **the cautionary tale** — technically legal ≠ soci
          +ally licensed; *centralize-and-trust without individual contro
          +l fails*. This is the negative-space proof for our approach.  
      646 +                                                              
      647 +**15. UK NHS — Google DeepMind / Royal Free (2015–2017)**     
      648 +- *Problem:* build an AKI (kidney) alert app (Streams).       
      649 +- *Solution:* transferred 1.6 M identifiable patient records t
          +o DeepMind.                                                   
      650 +- *Outcome:* UK ICO ruled the data-sharing **breached the Data
          + Protection Act (2017)**. `[SRC: UK ICO, 2017]`               
      651 +- *Lesson:* even well-intentioned centralization by a sophisti
          +cated AI lab triggers regulatory failure — compute-to-data wou
          +ld have avoided it.                                           
      652 +                                                              
      653 +**16. Genomics — federated genomic analysis (e.g., GA4GH, Beac
          +on)**                                                         
      654 +- *Problem:* genomic cohorts are too small at any one site; ge
          +nomes are maximally identifying.                              
      655 +- *Solution:* federated/query-based genomic data sharing (Glob
          +al Alliance for Genomics & Health; Beacon protocol with privac
          +y controls).                                                  
      656 +- *Technology:* federated queries + DP/access control.        
      657 +- *Outcome:* enables rare-disease research without moving geno
          +mes.                                                          
      658 +- *Lesson:* the most sensitive data class demands PETs — and P
          +ETs deliver.                                                  
      659 +                                                              
      660 +**17. Roche/Genentech, Intel — federated learning for patholog
          +y/oncology**                                                  
      661 +- *Problem:* multi-site cancer research.                      
      662 +- *Solution:* Intel OpenFL + academic medical centers (e.g., P
          +enn Medicine's federated tumor-segmentation, FeTS).           
      663 +- *Outcome:* FeTS federated tumor-segmentation across dozens o
          +f institutions — one of the largest medical FL studies. `[SRC:
          + Pati et al., Nature Communications, 2022]`                   
      664 +- *Lesson:* federated medical AI scales to tens of institution
          +s with measurable accuracy gains.                             
      665 +                                                              
      666 +## C. Financial services                                      
      667 +                                                              
      668 +**18. Mastercard — data responsibility & PET investment**     
      669 +- *Problem:* use transaction data for fraud/insights while pro
          +tecting cardholders.                                          
      670 +- *Solution:* data-responsibility principles; acquisitions (e.
          +g., Ekata for identity); tokenization of card numbers.        
      671 +- *Technology:* tokenization, network tokens, analytics govern
          +ance.                                                         
      672 +- *Outcome:* tokenization now protects a large share of digita
          +l transactions.                                               
      673 +- *Lesson:* payments already runs on "don't expose the raw val
          +ue" (tokenization) — a proven pattern.                        
      674 +                                                              
      675 +**19. Visa — network tokenization & AI fraud (Visa Advanced Au
          +thorization)**                                                
      676 +- *Problem:* real-time fraud detection across a global network
          +.                                                             
      677 +- *Solution:* tokenization + centralized-but-governed AI scori
          +ng.                                                           
      678 +- *Outcome:* billions in fraud prevented annually `[APPROX]`. 
      679 +- *Lesson:* the *value* of cross-institution fraud signal is e
          +normous — a federated cross-bank fraud network is a clear oppo
          +rtunity (see §11).                                            
      680 +                                                              
      681 +**20. Swift — federated/collaborative fraud signals**         
      682 +- *Problem:* cross-border payment fraud spans many banks.     
      683 +- *Solution:* Swift exploring collaborative/federated analytic
          +s for anomaly detection.                                      
      684 +- *Lesson:* interbank collaborative AI is an active, funded fr
          +ontier.                                                       
      685 +                                                              
      686 +**21. Banks — SMPC/PSI for AML & shared KYC (e.g., regulator p
          +ilots)**                                                      
      687 +- *Problem:* money laundering spans institutions; each bank se
          +es only fragments.                                            
      688 +- *Solution:* privacy-preserving information sharing (e.g., **
          +UK/Singapore MAS** projects; **COSMIC** platform in Singapore,
          + 2024).                                                       
      689 +- *Technology:* secure information-sharing + governance.      
      690 +- *Outcome:* Singapore's **COSMIC** (launched 2024) lets banks
          + share customer risk info to fight financial crime under a gov
          +erned platform. `[SRC: MAS COSMIC, 2024]`                     
      691 +- *Lesson:* regulators themselves are building collaborative-d
          +ata platforms — strong policy tailwind.                       
      692 +                                                              
      693 +**22. Boston Women's Workforce Council — MPC wage-gap study** 
      694 +- *Problem:* measure gender/racial pay gaps without any employ
          +er revealing salaries.                                        
      695 +- *Solution:* SMPC computes aggregate statistics across employ
          +ers.                                                          
      696 +- *Technology:* MPC (BU/Sharemind).                           
      697 +- *Outcome:* repeated annual studies; real policy insight from
          + data no one would otherwise share. `[SRC: BU/Boston WWC]`    
      698 +- *Lesson:* MPC unlocks socially valuable statistics from mutu
          +ally-distrustful parties.                                     
      699 +                                                              
      700 +## D. Public digital infrastructure (the India Stack & global 
          +peers)                                                        
      701 +                                                              
      702 +**23. India — Aadhaar**                                       
      703 +- *Problem:* give 1.4 B people verifiable digital identity.   
      704 +- *Solution:* biometric digital ID + authentication APIs.     
      705 +- *Technology:* biometric dedup, auth APIs; later privacy deba
          +tes (SC "right to privacy" judgment 2017; data-minimization ma
          +ndates).                                                      
      706 +- *Outcome:* **~1.3 B+ enrolled** — world's largest digital ID
          + `[APPROX]`.                                                  
      707 +- *Lesson:* population-scale identity rails exist in India — t
          +he substrate for individual-controlled data sharing. (Also a *
          +cautionary* case on centralization/privacy — informing our dec
          +entralized-control design.)                                   
      708 +                                                              
      709 +**24. India — UPI**                                           
      710 +- *Problem:* interoperable real-time payments.                
      711 +- *Solution:* Unified Payments Interface — an open protocol la
          +yer.                                                          
      712 +- *Outcome:* **>10–18 billion transactions/month** in 2024–25 
          +`[APPROX]`; the world's largest real-time payment system.     
      713 +- *Lesson:* open protocol + consent layer + population scale =
          + the India Stack playbook we ride.                            
      714 +                                                              
      715 +**25. India — Account Aggregator (AA) / DEPA**                
      716 +- *Problem:* let individuals share financial data across insti
          +tutions **with consent and control**.                         
      717 +- *Solution:* consent-artifact framework (RBI-regulated AAs) —
          + machine-readable, granular, time-bound, revocable consent.   
      718 +- *Technology:* **DEPA** (Data Empowerment & Protection Archit
          +ecture) + consent API.                                        
      719 +- *Outcome:* **100 M+ consents by Aug 2024; ~120 M linked acco
          +unts by Dec 2024** (up from 39 M a year earlier); ~570+ regula
          +ted entities; enabling ₹1.6 lakh crore of loans. `[SRC: Sahama
          +ti / Medianama, 2024]`                                        
      720 +- *Lesson:* **this is the closest live analogue to our solutio
          +n's control plane, at population scale** — proof individual-co
          +ntrolled data sharing works, and a rail to build on. Our play:
          + extend the DEPA pattern *beyond finance* (health, and into AI
          + training) with stronger compute-side PETs.                   
      721 +                                                              
      722 +**26. India — DigiLocker**                                    
      723 +- *Problem:* let citizens hold & share official documents digi
          +tally.                                                        
      724 +- *Solution:* government-issued verifiable documents in a citi
          +zen-controlled locker.                                        
      725 +- *Outcome:* **hundreds of millions of users** `[APPROX]`.    
      726 +- *Lesson:* citizens will use a self-custody credential wallet
          + at scale — de-risks the SSI/VC UX assumption.                
      727 +                                                              
      728 +**27. India — ONDC**                                          
      729 +- *Problem:* break platform monopolies in e-commerce via open 
          +protocol.                                                     
      730 +- *Solution:* Open Network for Digital Commerce — unbundles bu
          +yers/sellers/logistics.                                       
      731 +- *Lesson:* India's "protocol not platform" philosophy is the 
          +strategic template for a data-sharing network.                
      732 +                                                              
      733 +**28. India — CoWIN (vaccine platform)**                      
      734 +- *Problem:* orchestrate 2 B+ vaccine doses with identity + re
          +cords.                                                        
      735 +- *Outcome:* massive scale; also a 2023 data-leak controversy.
      736 +- *Lesson:* scale is achievable; **centralized PII is a liabil
          +ity** — reinforces decentralized-control design.              
      737 +                                                              
      738 +**29. Estonia — X-Road & digital government**                 
      739 +- *Problem:* connect government registries without a central m
          +ega-database.                                                 
      740 +- *Solution:* **X-Road** — a decentralized, secure data-exchan
          +ge layer where data stays in source registries and is queried 
          +with logged, authorized access; citizens can **see who accesse
          +d their data**.                                               
      741 +- *Technology:* distributed data exchange + KSI blockchain for
          + log integrity + strong eID.                                  
      742 +- *Outcome:* ~99% of public services online; citizen-visible a
          +ccess logs — **individual oversight built in**. `[SRC: e-Eston
          +ia]`                                                          
      743 +- *Lesson:* **the gold-standard reference architecture** — dat
          +a stays distributed, every access is logged and citizen-visibl
          +e. Directly informs our audit-ledger design.                  
      744 +                                                              
      745 +**30. Singapore — Smart Nation & PDPC PET Sandbox**           
      746 +- *Problem:* enable data innovation with trust.               
      747 +- *Solution:* national digital identity (Singpass), **PDPC's P
          +ET Sandbox** (2022→) to trial privacy tech, model AI governanc
          +e framework.                                                  
      748 +- *Outcome:* government-blessed PET experimentation environmen
          +t.                                                            
      749 +- *Lesson:* progressive regulators create sandboxes → a launch
          + channel and credibility source.                              
      750 +                                                              
      751 +**31. EU — Gaia-X & European data spaces**                    
      752 +- *Problem:* reduce dependence on US clouds; enable sovereign,
          + sector-specific data sharing (health, mobility).             
      753 +- *Solution:* federated data-infrastructure standards + sector
          +al "data spaces."                                             
      754 +- *Technology:* federated catalogs, policy-enforced sharing, P
          +ETs.                                                          
      755 +- *Outcome:* early but well-funded; regulatory push via Data A
          +ct/Data Governance Act.                                       
      756 +- *Lesson:* Europe is legislating data-sharing infrastructure 
          +into existence — a market being *created by law*.             
      757 +                                                              
      758 +**32. EU — Digital Identity Wallet (eIDAS 2.0)**              
      759 +- *Problem:* give every EU citizen a portable, user-controlled
          + identity wallet.                                             
      760 +- *Solution:* mandated EUDI Wallet with verifiable credentials
          + + selective disclosure.                                      
      761 +- *Outcome:* rollout 2024–2026; ~450 M potential users.       
      762 +- *Lesson:* the SSI/VC control layer is becoming **legally man
          +dated** at continental scale — huge tailwind for our control p
          +lane.                                                         
      763 +                                                              
      764 +**33. World Bank — ID4D & MOSIP**                             
      765 +- *Problem:* ~850 M people lack legal ID; enable inclusion wit
          +h privacy.                                                    
      766 +- *Solution:* **MOSIP** (open-source modular ID platform, used
          + by Philippines, Ethiopia, Morocco) + World Bank ID4D.        
      767 +- *Technology:* open-source digital ID with privacy-by-design.
      768 +- *Outcome:* multiple national IDs built on MOSIP.            
      769 +- *Lesson:* privacy-preserving digital-ID infrastructure is a 
          +global public-good movement — our control plane can interopera
          +te.                                                           
      770 +                                                              
      771 +## Cross-case synthesis (what the 33 cases jointly prove)     
      772 +                                                              
      773 +| Claim | Cases that prove it |                               
      774 +|---|---|                                                     
      775 +| PET-based collaboration **outperforms** siloed AI | #6 (EXAM
          +), #12 (MELLODDY), #17 (FeTS) |                               
      776 +| PETs are **production-real at scale** | #1 (Gboard), #2 (App
          +le DP), #4/#5 (confidential computing) |                      
      777 +| Individual-controlled sharing **works at population scale** 
          +| #25 (AA/DEPA), #26 (DigiLocker), #29 (Estonia), #32 (EUDI) |
      778 +| **Centralize-and-trust fails** (technical + social) | #14 (C
          +are.data), #15 (DeepMind/Royal Free), #28 (CoWIN leak) |      
      779 +| Competitors **will** collaborate under crypto guarantees | #
          +12 (MELLODDY), #21 (COSMIC) |                                 
      780 +| Privacy is a **fundable, monetizable** category | #11 (Owkin
          + US$334 M), plus §5 |                                         
      781 +| Regulators are **building/mandating** the rails | #21 (COSMI
          +C), #25 (DEPA), #30 (Singapore), #31/#32 (EU) |               
      782 +                                                              
      783 +**The decisive narrative for slides:** privacy-preserving coll
          +aborative AI is *not speculative* — it already beats siloed AI
          + on real tasks (EXAM/MELLODDY), already runs at population sca
          +le (DEPA/Estonia), and the alternative (centralize-and-trust) 
          +has *already failed publicly* (Care.data/DeepMind). The gap is
          + a **usable, individual-controlled, cross-vertical product** —
          + which no case above fully delivers.

      905 +                                                              
      906 +## 5.10 Illustrative deep-dive SWOTs (3 representative rivals)
      907 +                                                              
      908 +**Owkin (the FL leader we'd cite most)**                      
      909 +- *Strengths:* pharma validation, US$334 M raised, Substra OSS
          +, deep bio/AI talent.                                         
      910 +- *Weaknesses:* narrow to life sciences; heavy services; not i
          +ndividual-facing.                                             
      911 +- *Opportunities:* expand FL to other verticals.              
      912 +- *Threats:* NVIDIA/cloud commoditizing FL; pharma building in
          +-house.                                                       
      913 +- *Gap for us:* Owkin serves institutions, not **individuals**
          + — no consumer control plane; and it's health-only.           
      914 +                                                              
      915 +**OneTrust (the compliance incumbent)**                       
      916 +- *Strengths:* enormous distribution (½ of F500), brand, bread
          +th (privacy/GRC/AI governance).                               
      917 +- *Weaknesses:* **compliance documentation, not privacy-preser
          +ving computation** — it records consent, it doesn't enforce it
          + at compute time.                                             
      918 +- *Opportunities:* bolt PETs on via acquisition.              
      919 +- *Threats:* PET-native entrants making "documented consent" l
          +ook hollow.                                                   
      920 +- *Gap for us:* OneTrust proves the *market pays for privacy t
          +ooling*, but leaves the **actual data-in-use protection + indi
          +vidual enforcement** unbuilt — precisely our wedge.           
      921 +                                                              
      922 +**Snowflake/Databricks clean rooms (the platform threat)**    
      923 +- *Strengths:* own the data gravity; frictionless for existing
          + customers.                                                   
      924 +- *Weaknesses:* operator-trust model; not individual-facing; l
          +ock-in to one cloud/warehouse.                                
      925 +- *Threats to us:* they could bundle "good enough" collaborati
          +on.                                                           
      926 +- *Gap for us:* cross-org, cross-cloud, **individual-controlle
          +d** collaboration with formal guarantees — neutrality they can
          +'t offer.                                                     
      927 +                                                              
      928 +## 5.11 The strategic gap (synthesis)                         
      929 +                                                              
      930 +```mermaid                                                    
      931 +quadrantChart                                                 
      932 +    title Competitive positioning                             
      933 +    x-axis "Institution-facing" --> "Individual-facing"       
      934 +    y-axis "Compliance/documentation" --> "Cryptographic enfor
          +cement"                                                       
      935 +    quadrant-1 "OPEN WHITE SPACE"                             
      936 +    quadrant-2 "PET compute vendors"                          
      937 +    quadrant-3 "Compliance software"                          
      938 +    quadrant-4 "Consent/SSI tools"                            
      939 +    OneTrust: [0.2, 0.2]                                      
      940 +    BigID: [0.25, 0.3]                                        
      941 +    Duality: [0.3, 0.8]                                       
      942 +    Owkin: [0.35, 0.75]                                       
      943 +    Snowflake_CR: [0.3, 0.55]                                 
      944 +    Zama: [0.35, 0.9]                                         
      945 +    AA_DEPA: [0.8, 0.5]                                       
      946 +    EUDI_Wallet: [0.85, 0.55]                                 
      947 +    Our_venture: [0.8, 0.85]                                  
      948 +```                                                           
      949 +                                                              
      950 +**The white space (upper-right):** *individual-facing* + *cryp
          +tographically enforced*. Every strong competitor sits in one a
          +xis:                                                          
      951 +- PET-compute vendors (Duality, Owkin, Zama) = strong enforcem
          +ent, **institution-only**.                                    
      952 +- Compliance vendors (OneTrust, BigID) = individual-*touching*
          + (DSARs) but **documentation, not enforcement**.              
      953 +- Consent networks (AA/DEPA, EUDI) = individual-facing + real 
          +enforcement, but **domain-limited** (finance/identity) and **n
          +ot applied to AI training/collaboration**.                    
      954 +                                                              
      955 +**Our position:** compose PET-grade enforcement *with* an indi
          +vidual control plane, applied to *collaborative AI*, launched 
          +on India's consent rails. No incumbent occupies this quadrant.
      956 +                                                              
      957 +**Failed-startup lessons (survivorship-corrected):** TripleBli
          +nd (wound down 2024), Hazy (wound down 2024), Cape Privacy (pi
          +voted). Common failure modes: (a) selling *technology* not *ou
          +tcomes*; (b) too-heavy crypto (HE) without a killer use-case; 
          +(c) horizontal platform with no vertical wedge; (d) enterprise
          + sales cycles outrunning cash. **Our roadmap (§14) is explicit
          +ly designed to avoid all four** via a vertical wedge, defense-
          +in-depth (not HE-only), outcome-based selling, and regulation-
          +driven urgency.

       961 +# 6. PATENT LANDSCAPE                                        
       962 +                                                             
       963 +## 6.1 Where the patents concentrate                         
       964 +                                                             
       965 +Patent activity in PETs has grown sharply since ~2017 (post-f
           +ederated-learning). Concentration by assignee and domain:    
       966 +                                                             
       967 +| Domain | Heavy filers | Character of claims |              
       968 +|---|---|---|                                                
       969 +| **Federated learning** | Google, Apple, WeBank, Samsung, IB
           +M, Intel, Ping An | System/method claims on aggregation, secu
           +re aggregation, communication-efficient FL, personalization, 
           +on-device training |                                         
       970 +| **Differential privacy** | Google, Apple, Microsoft | Local
           +/central DP mechanisms, privacy-budget management, DP telemet
           +ry |                                                         
       971 +| **Homomorphic encryption** | IBM, Microsoft, Intel, Zama, D
           +uality | FHE bootstrapping, hardware acceleration, encrypted 
           +inference |                                                  
       972 +| **Confidential computing / TEE** | Intel (SGX/TDX), AMD (SE
           +V), ARM, NVIDIA, Microsoft | Enclave architecture, remote att
           +estation, confidential GPU/accelerator |                     
       973 +| **Secure MPC** | Various + academic | Secret sharing protoc
           +ols, private set intersection, garbled circuits |            
       974 +| **Verifiable credentials / SSI** | Microsoft, IBM, Masterca
           +rd, Workday, blockchain firms | Credential issuance/verificat
           +ion, selective disclosure, revocation |                      
       975 +| **ZKP** | Blockchain firms (StarkWare, zkSync/Matter Labs, 
           +Polygon), IBM | Succinct proofs, circuit compilation, private
           + transactions |                                              
       976 +                                                             
       977 +## 6.2 Notable / representative patent themes                
       978 +                                                             
       979 +- **Google** — foundational FL and secure-aggregation filings
           + (McMahan-lineage); DP telemetry.                            
       980 +- **Apple** — local DP data-collection patents; on-device lea
           +rning.                                                       
       981 +- **Intel/AMD** — the enclave & attestation patent thicket un
           +derpinning all confidential computing.                       
       982 +- **IBM** — long HE patent history (Gentry-era) + confidentia
           +l computing.                                                 
       983 +- **WeBank / Ping An (China)** — aggressive FL patenting (FAT
           +E ecosystem) — China leads in FL patent *volume*.            
       984 +- **Mastercard/Visa** — tokenization + verifiable-credential 
           ++ privacy-preserving identity patents.                       
       985 +                                                             
       986 +## 6.3 White spaces & innovation gaps (where patents are thin
           + → opportunity)                                              
       987 +                                                             
       988 +1. **Composition/orchestration IP** — patents cluster on *ind
           +ividual* techniques (FL, DP, TEE) but **the orchestration lay
           +er that composes them with policy-driven selection** is compa
           +ratively open. *(Our core novelty could sit here.)*          
       989 +2. **Individual-facing consent-to-compute binding** — cryptog
           +raphically binding a *revocable individual consent artifact* 
           +to a *specific model-training event*, with audit — thin paten
           +t coverage outside finance (AA).                             
       990 +3. **Consent revocation → machine unlearning linkage** — turn
           +ing a revoked consent into an enforceable model-update/unlear
           +ning action is an open research+IP frontier.                 
       991 +4. **Cross-vertical consent portability** — a credential that
           + governs data use across health *and* finance *and* AI traini
           +ng simultaneously.                                           
       992 +5. **DP-budget accounting as a governed, auditable ledger** a
           +cross multiple collaborations.                               
       993 +6. **UX/interaction patents** for non-expert privacy-budget a
           +nd consent management (design patents + method claims).      
       994 +                                                             
       995 +**Freedom-to-operate note:** the deep crypto primitives are h
           +eavily patented *and* heavily open-sourced (permissive licens
           +es on SEAL, OpenFHE, OpenFL, Flower, PySyft). Strategy: **bui
           +ld on permissively-licensed OSS primitives; file our own IP o
           +n the orchestration + individual-control + audit layer** wher
           +e white space exists. This is both a legal-risk-mitigation an
           +d a moat strategy.                                           
       996 +
