       998 +                                                             
       999 +# 7. ACADEMIC RESEARCH REVIEW (100+ papers, clustered)       
      1000 +                                                             
      1001 +> Rather than list 100 papers as flat citations (low signal),
           + they are **clustered into 12 research threads**, each with s
           +eminal + recent works, key findings, and open problems. Count
           +s below exceed 100 distinct works referenced. Foundational pa
           +pers are `[SRC]`; the broader bodies are summarized.         
      1002 +                                                             
      1003 +## Cluster 1 — Differential Privacy foundations (≈12 papers) 
      1004 +- Dwork et al., "Calibrating Noise to Sensitivity" (TCC 2006)
           + — birth of DP. `[SRC]`                                      
      1005 +- Dwork & Roth, "The Algorithmic Foundations of DP" (2014) — 
           +the textbook. `[SRC]`                                        
      1006 +- Abadi et al., "Deep Learning with Differential Privacy / DP
           +-SGD" (CCS 2016). `[SRC]`                                    
      1007 +- McSherry & Talwar (exponential mechanism); Mironov (Rényi D
           +P, 2017); Bun & Steinke (concentrated DP).                   
      1008 +- **Finding:** DP gives the only composable, mathematical pri
           +vacy guarantee. **Open problem:** setting ε in practice; util
           +ity loss on high-dimensional/deep models; ε interpretability 
           +for non-experts and regulators.                              
      1009 +                                                             
      1010 +## Cluster 2 — Federated Learning systems (≈15 papers)       
      1011 +- McMahan et al., "Communication-Efficient Learning… / FedAvg
           +" (AISTATS 2017). `[SRC]`                                    
      1012 +- Kairouz et al., "Advances and Open Problems in Federated Le
           +arning" (2019/2021) — the 100-author survey; **essential**. `
           +[SRC]`                                                       
      1013 +- Bonawitz et al., "Practical Secure Aggregation" (CCS 2017);
           + "Towards Federated Learning at Scale" (2019).               
      1014 +- Li et al., FedProx (non-IID); Karimireddy et al., SCAFFOLD;
           + personalization (Ditto, pFedMe).                            
      1015 +- **Finding:** FL works at scale but struggles with non-IID d
           +ata, systems heterogeneity, and communication. **Open problem
           +:** convergence under extreme heterogeneity; fairness across 
           +clients; incentive design.                                   
      1016 +                                                             
      1017 +## Cluster 3 — Privacy attacks on ML (≈12 papers) — *why FL a
           +lone is insufficient*                                        
      1018 +- Shokri et al., "Membership Inference Attacks" (S&P 2017). `
           +[SRC]`                                                       
      1019 +- Carlini et al., "Extracting Training Data from LLMs" (USENI
           +X 2021); "Quantifying Memorization" (2022). `[SRC]`          
      1020 +- Zhu et al., "Deep Leakage from Gradients" (NeurIPS 2019) — 
           +reconstructs data from gradients. `[SRC]`                    
      1021 +- Fredrikson et al., model inversion; Nasr et al., white-box 
           +membership inference.                                        
      1022 +- **Finding:** models leak training data; gradients leak inpu
           +ts. **Implication (central to our thesis):** FL must be combi
           +ned with DP and/or TEE. **Open problem:** tight, practical le
           +akage bounds; auditing deployed models.                      
      1023 +                                                             
      1024 +## Cluster 4 — Secure Multi-Party Computation (≈10 papers)   
      1025 +- Yao, "Protocols for Secure Computations / garbled circuits"
           + (FOCS 1982). `[SRC]`                                        
      1026 +- Goldreich-Micali-Wigderson (GMW, 1987); BGW; SPDZ (Damgård 
           +et al., 2012); ABY/ABY3.                                     
      1027 +- Private Set Intersection line (Pinkas et al.).             
      1028 +- **Finding:** MPC is secure and exact but communication-boun
           +d. **Open problem:** scaling to deep learning; malicious-secu
           +rity efficiency.                                             
      1029 +                                                             
      1030 +## Cluster 5 — Homomorphic Encryption (≈10 papers)           
      1031 +- Gentry, "Fully Homomorphic Encryption Using Ideal Lattices"
           + (STOC 2009). `[SRC]`                                        
      1032 +- BGV, BFV, CKKS (approximate arithmetic — key for ML), TFHE 
           +(fast bootstrapping).                                        
      1033 +- CryptoNets (Gilad-Bachrach et al., 2016) — encrypted infere
           +nce.                                                         
      1034 +- **Finding:** FHE is feasible but slow; CKKS + hardware acce
           +l narrowing the gap. **Open problem:** practical FHE for trai
           +ning; standardization (ISO/IEC + HomomorphicEncryption.org). 
      1035 +                                                             
      1036 +## Cluster 6 — Trusted Execution / Confidential Computing (≈8
           + papers)                                                     
      1037 +- Costan & Devadas, "Intel SGX Explained" (2016). `[SRC]`    
      1038 +- Side-channel attacks: Foreshadow, Spectre, SGAxe, Plundervo
           +lt — the security caveats.                                   
      1039 +- Confidential ML: Chiron, Slalom, confidential GPU work.    
      1040 +- **Finding:** TEEs give low-overhead data-in-use protection 
           +but face side-channel + trust-in-vendor issues. **Open proble
           +m:** verifiable, side-channel-resistant enclaves; attestation
           + standardization.                                            
      1041 +                                                             
      1042 +## Cluster 7 — Federated + DP + Crypto composition (≈10 paper
           +s)                                                           
      1043 +- Truex et al., "A Hybrid Approach to Privacy-Preserving FL" 
           +(2019).                                                      
      1044 +- DP-FedAvg (McMahan et al., 2018, "Learning Differentially P
           +rivate Recurrent Language Models"). `[SRC]`                  
      1045 +- Secure aggregation + DP; TEE-backed FL.                    
      1046 +- **Finding:** composition is where real systems live. **Open
           + problem:** end-to-end guarantees; usable defaults; performan
           +ce.                                                          
      1047 +                                                             
      1048 +## Cluster 8 — Synthetic data & generative privacy (≈8 papers
           +)                                                            
      1049 +- Xie et al., DP-GAN; Jordon et al., PATE-GAN (2019). `[SRC]`
      1050 +- Papernot et al., PATE (2017/2018) — private aggregation of 
           +teacher ensembles. `[SRC]`                                   
      1051 +- Evaluations showing synthetic data can leak (membership inf
           +erence on generators).                                       
      1052 +- **Finding:** DP-synthetic data can share statistics safely 
           +if trained with DP. **Open problem:** fidelity–privacy tradeo
           +ff; auditing "is this really anonymous?".                    
      1053 +                                                             
      1054 +## Cluster 9 — Machine Unlearning & the right to be forgotten
           + (≈8 papers)                                                 
      1055 +- Cao & Yang, "Towards Making Systems Forget" (2015); Bourtou
           +le et al., "Machine Unlearning / SISA" (2021). `[SRC]`       
      1056 +- Approximate unlearning; influence functions; unlearning for
           + LLMs (2023–25).                                             
      1057 +- **Finding:** removing a data point's influence from a train
           +ed model is hard; exact unlearning is expensive. **Open probl
           +em (huge & directly relevant):** linking *consent revocation*
           + to *model unlearning* efficiently — a core research gap our 
           +platform surfaces.                                           
      1058 +                                                             
      1059 +## Cluster 10 — Self-Sovereign Identity / VCs / ZKP for ident
           +ity (≈8 papers)                                              
      1060 +- W3C DID-Core & Verifiable Credentials Data Model (specs). `
           +[SRC]`                                                       
      1061 +- Goldwasser-Micali-Rackoff, "Knowledge Complexity of Interac
           +tive Proof Systems" (1985) — ZKP origin. `[SRC]`             
      1062 +- zk-SNARKs (Groth16), zk-STARKs (Ben-Sasson et al.); anonymo
           +us credentials (Camenisch-Lysyanskaya).                      
      1063 +- **Finding:** cryptographic selective disclosure is mature e
           +nough for consent. **Open problem:** UX, key recovery, revoca
           +tion at scale, interoperability.                             
      1064 +                                                             
      1065 +## Cluster 11 — Privacy economics, consent & contextual integ
           +rity (≈8 papers)                                             
      1066 +- Nissenbaum, "Privacy as Contextual Integrity" (2004). `[SRC
           +]`                                                           
      1067 +- Acquisti et al., "The Economics of Privacy" (J. Econ. Lit. 
           +2016) — privacy paradox, willingness-to-pay. `[SRC]`         
      1068 +- Solove, "Privacy Self-Management and the Consent Dilemma" (
           +2013). `[SRC]`                                               
      1069 +- **Finding:** notice-and-consent is broken; humans can't sel
           +f-manage privacy at scale. **Open problem:** consent that is 
           +meaningful *and* usable — argues for automated, machine-enfor
           +ced consent (our control plane).                             
      1070 +                                                             
      1071 +## Cluster 12 — Data markets, incentives & governance (≈8 pap
           +ers)                                                         
      1072 +- "Data Shapley" (Ghorbani & Zou, 2019) — valuing data contri
           +butions. `[SRC]`                                             
      1073 +- Data dignity / data-as-labor (Lanier & Weyl); data trusts/c
           +ooperatives literature.                                      
      1074 +- Federated incentive mechanisms; data-markets design.       
      1075 +- **Finding:** without fair value/incentive design, data coll
           +aboration stalls. **Open problem:** pricing/attribution for f
           +ederated contributions; sustainable governance (data trusts).
      1076 +                                                             
      1077 +## 7.1 Meta-findings across all clusters                     
      1078 +                                                             
      1079 +1. **No single PET is sufficient** — the literature converges
           + on *composition* (Cluster 3 + 7). This validates our defense
           +-in-depth architecture.                                      
      1080 +2. **The individual-control + unlearning + consent-enforcemen
           +t linkage is the least-solved area** (Clusters 9, 11) — the r
           +ichest research white space and our differentiation.         
      1081 +3. **Usability, not feasibility, is the binding constraint** 
           +— the crypto works; the UX/economics don't yet (Clusters 10, 
           +11, 12).                                                     
      1082 +4. **Auditing/verification of privacy guarantees in deployed 
           +systems** is immature — a product opportunity (privacy attest
           +ation/observability).                                        
      1083 +                                                             
      1084 +## 7.2 Open research problems → product features mapping     
      1085 +                                                             
      1086 +| Open problem | Product feature it becomes |                
      1087 +|---|---|                                                    
      1088 +| Setting/interpreting ε | A "privacy budget dashboard" with 
           +plain-language ε |                                           
      1089 +| Consent revocation → unlearning | "Revoke & forget" workflo
           +w tied to model retraining/unlearning |                      
      1090 +| Non-IID FL convergence | Robust cross-silo training default
           +s |                                                          
      1091 +| Auditing deployed privacy | Privacy-attestation & lineage o
           +bservability |                                               
      1092 +| Data valuation/incentives | Contribution accounting / rewar
           +ds for data providers |                                      
      1093 +                                                             
      1094 +---                                                          
      1095 +                                                             
      1096 +# 8. GOVERNMENT POLICIES & REGULATION                        
      1097 +                                                             
      1098 +> The regulatory environment is the single biggest tailwind. 
           +Below: each major regime, what it mandates, and the **implica
           +tion for our solution** (how compliance becomes the product).
      1099 +                                                             
      1100 +## 8.1 India — DPDP Act 2023 + DPDP Rules 2025 (our home regi
           +me)                                                          
      1101 +- **Act passed Aug 2023; Rules notified 13–14 Nov 2025.** Pha
           +sed: Data Protection Board stood up Nov 2025; consent-manager
           + registration from Nov 2026; **core obligations (consent, not
           +ice, security) effective ~May 2027.** `[SRC: MeitY/PIB, 2025]
           +`                                                            
      1102 +- **Key concepts:** *Data Principal* (individual), *Data Fidu
           +ciary* (controller), *Consent Manager* (a registered intermed
           +iary managing consent — a role our platform can occupy), *Sig
           +nificant Data Fiduciary* (extra obligations).                
      1103 +- **Rights:** access, correction, erasure, grievance redressa
           +l, nominate.                                                 
      1104 +- **Penalties:** up to **₹250 crore** per instance (~US$30 M)
           +. `[SRC: DPDP Act 2023]`                                     
      1105 +- **Implication:** the **Consent Manager** construct is a *le
           +gally defined product category* we can build into. DPDP's con
           +sent + erasure mandate is exactly our control plane. **This r
           +egime effectively invites our product into existence.**      
      1106 +                                                             
      1107 +## 8.2 EU — GDPR (2018)                                      
      1108 +- Rights: access, erasure ("right to be forgotten," Art. 17),
           + portability (Art. 20), rectification; lawful-basis + purpose
           + limitation + data minimization; DPIAs; privacy-by-design (Ar
           +t. 25).                                                      
      1109 +- **Penalties:** up to €20 M or **4% of global turnover**. **
           +Cumulative fines >€7.1 B by 2025**; ~€1.1–1.2 B in 2025 alone
           +; largest single: €1.2 B Meta (2023), €530 M TikTok (2025). `
           +[SRC: CMS GDPR Enforcement Tracker; Surfshark, 2025]`        
      1110 +- **Implication:** Art. 25 (privacy-by-design) + Art. 17 (era
           +sure) + Art. 20 (portability) are literal feature requirement
           +s our architecture satisfies natively. PETs are increasingly 
           +regulator-recommended (EDPB, ENISA reports on PETs/pseudonymi
           +zation).                                                     
      1111 +                                                             
      1112 +## 8.3 EU — AI Act (2024, phased 2025–2027)                  
      1113 +- **Timeline:** prohibited practices + AI literacy from **2 F
           +eb 2025**; GPAI obligations + penalties framework from **2 Au
           +g 2025**; high-risk obligations **2 Aug 2026** (a 2026 "Digit
           +al Omnibus" proposal may delay Annex III high-risk to Dec 202
           +7). `[SRC: EU AI Act / EDPS timeline, 2025]`                 
      1114 +- **Penalties:** up to €35 M or **7% of global turnover**.   
      1115 +- **Requirements (high-risk):** data governance, logging, hum
           +an oversight, transparency, risk management.                 
      1116 +- **Implication:** "data governance + logging + transparency"
           + for high-risk AI (health, credit, employment) = our audit le
           +dger + consent lineage. AI Act + GDPR *converge* on exactly o
           +ur value prop.                                               
      1117 +                                                             
      1118 +## 8.4 US — sectoral + state patchwork                       
      1119 +- **HIPAA** (health): Privacy & Security Rules; de-identifica
           +tion (Safe Harbor / Expert Determination). PETs enable HIPAA-
           +compliant multi-site research.                               
      1120 +- **CCPA/CPRA** (California) + **~20 state laws** by 2026 (Vi
           +rginia VCDPA, Colorado CPA, etc.): access/delete/opt-out of s
           +ale.                                                         
      1121 +- **GLBA** (finance), **FERPA** (education), **COPPA** (child
           +ren).                                                        
      1122 +- **Implication:** fragmentation = compliance pain = demand f
           +or a unifying, PET-based control layer. No federal privacy la
           +w yet (as of 2026) → state complexity persists.              
      1123 +                                                             
      1124 +## 8.5 US — NIST frameworks (soft law, high influence)       
      1125 +- **NIST Privacy Framework** (2020); **NIST AI Risk Managemen
           +t Framework (AI RMF 1.0, 2023)** + Generative AI profile (202
           +4); NIST work on PETs & DP guidelines (SP 800-226).          
      1126 +- **Implication:** NIST AI RMF is the de-facto enterprise gov
           +ernance standard — mapping our platform to AI RMF functions (
           +Govern/Map/Measure/Manage) is a strong enterprise-sales artif
           +act.                                                         
      1127 +                                                             
      1128 +## 8.6 Other regimes                                         
      1129 +- **China — PIPL (2021)** + Data Security Law + data-localiza
           +tion: strict; forces on-shore/federated compute → drives PET 
           +adoption.                                                    
      1130 +- **Brazil — LGPD**; **Canada — PIPEDA / CPPA**; **UK — UK GD
           +PR + DPDI reforms**; **Singapore — PDPA + Model AI Governance
           + + PET Sandbox**; **Japan — APPI**; **Korea — PIPA**.        
      1131 +- **ISO/IEC standards:** ISO/IEC 27001/27701 (privacy info mg
           +mt), ISO/IEC 27018 (cloud PII), ISO/IEC 20889 (de-identificat
           +ion), emerging ISO/IEC AI standards (42001 AI management syst
           +em), FHE standardization efforts.                            
      1132 +- **OECD AI Principles (2019, updated 2024)** + **G20 AI Prin
           +ciples**; **UNESCO Recommendation on AI Ethics (2021)**; **WE
           +F** data-for-common-good initiatives.                        
      1133 +- **India AI Mission (IndiaAI, ~₹10,300 crore, 2024)** + Meit
           +Y's frameworks + NITI Aayog's "Responsible AI" & DEPA work.  
      1134 +                                                             
      1135 +## 8.7 Regulatory synthesis (the tailwind table)             
      1136 +                                                             
      1137 +| Regime | Core mandate | Maps to our feature |              
      1138 +|---|---|---|                                                
      1139 +| DPDP 2025 | Consent Manager, erasure, notice | Consent cont
           +rol plane (we *are* a Consent Manager) |                     
      1140 +| GDPR | Privacy-by-design, erasure, portability | Compute-to
           +-data + audit ledger |                                       
      1141 +| EU AI Act | Data governance, logging, transparency (high-ri
           +sk AI) | Consent lineage + attestation |                     
      1142 +| HIPAA | De-identification, minimum necessary | Federated + 
           +DP for multi-site health AI |                                
      1143 +| NIST AI RMF | Govern/Map/Measure/Manage | Governance dashbo
           +ard |                                                        
      1144 +| PIPL | Localization | Federated/on-prem compute |          
      1145 +                                                             
      1146 +**Strategic read:** every major regime is independently conve
           +rging on *purpose limitation + minimization + individual righ
           +ts + auditability + AI governance*. Our architecture is the *
           +*technical common denominator** that satisfies all of them at
           + once. **Regulation is not a risk to this venture; it is the 
           +demand generator.**

      1152 +## 9.1 Stakeholder map (who must say "yes")                  
      1153 +                                                             
      1154 +```mermaid

      1252 +                                                             
      1253 +| ERRC | Move |                                              
      1254 +|---|---|                                                    
      1255 +| **Eliminate** | Raw-data copying; blanket unread consent; o
           +perator-trust-only clean rooms |                             
      1256 +| **Reduce** | Crypto complexity exposed to users; enterprise
           + integration time; compliance ambiguity |                    
      1257 +| **Raise** | Individual control (revocable, auditable); form
           +al privacy guarantees; regulator legibility |                
      1258 +| **Create** | An individual-facing consent-to-compute contro
           +l plane; cross-vertical consent portability; "revoke & forget
           +" workflow; a neutral cross-org/cross-cloud collaboration fab
           +ric |                                                        
      1259 +                                                             
      1260 +**Blue-ocean statement:** move from the red ocean of *complia
           +nce software* and *institution-only clean rooms* to the uncon
           +tested space of **individual-controlled, cryptographically-en
           +forced collaborative AI**.

           +sitive; mitigate with vertical wedge + ROI proof |

           +hetic data, plain clean rooms, in-house builds |

           +d; no one owns the individual-control quadrant |

      1375 +                                                             
      1376 +## 11.2 Why the weak ideas were rejected (patterns)          
      1377 +                                                             
      1378 +- **Red-ocean** (7, 15, 24): established, commoditizing, weak
           + differentiation.                                            
      1379 +- **Tech-too-immature** (8, 29): FHE performance not there.  
      1380 +- **Feature-not-product** (28, 30, 9, 11): valuable *inside* 
           +the platform, not standalone businesses.                     
      1381 +- **GTM-too-hard-first** (4, 19, 22): right direction, wrong 
           +*starting* point (chicken-and-egg or long gov cycles).       
      1382 +                                                             
      1383 +## 11.3 The chosen solution (Direction #1, fully specified)  
      1384 +                                                             
      1385 +**Name (working):** *ConsentGrid* — a **privacy-preserving co
           +llaborative-AI platform with an individual-controlled consent
           + & audit plane.**                                            
      1386 +                                                             
      1387 +**One-sentence definition:** ConsentGrid lets regulated insti
           +tutions jointly train and query AI models on their combined d
           +ata **without moving or exposing raw records**, while every i
           +ndividual whose data is involved holds a **revocable, verifia
           +ble, auditable grant** over each use.                        
      1388 +                                                             
      1389 +**Three planes (from §3.14):**                               
      1390 +1. **Control plane (the differentiator):** individual consent
           + as verifiable credentials + selective disclosure (ZKP) + a t
           +amper-evident consent/audit ledger; a "Consent Manager" under
           + DPDP; a "revoke & forget" workflow.                         
      1391 +2. **Compute plane:** cross-silo **federated learning** execu
           +ted inside **TEEs/confidential compute**, with **differential
           + privacy** budgeting on outputs and **SMPC/PSI** for private 
           +joins.                                                       
      1392 +3. **Data plane:** raw data stays in each institution's envir
           +onment; nothing centralizes.                                 
      1393 +                                                             
      1394 +**Launch wedge:** **multi-hospital federated diagnostics in I
           +ndia** (Direction #3) — highest impact, clear ROI (better mod
           +els, publishable, EXAM/MELLODDY-proven), acute compliance pai
           +n (DPDP). Then expand to **cross-bank fraud/credit** (Directi
           +ons #2, #17) riding Account Aggregator rails.                
      1395 +                                                             
      1396 +**Why this one wins (the justification):**                   
      1397 +1. **Exact problem-statement fit (P=5):** "secure data sharin
           +g" (compute-to-data) + "collaborative AI development" (federa
           +ted training) + "individuals retain control" (consent/audit p
           +lane). Literally the three clauses.                          
      1398 +2. **Proven components (F=4):** every layer is production-rea
           +l (Gboard/EXAM/Census/Azure) — we compose, not invent from sc
           +ratch.                                                       
      1399 +3. **Uncontested position (D=5):** the individual-facing + cr
           +yptographically-enforced quadrant is empty (§5.11).          
      1400 +4. **Regulatory demand engine (B=4):** DPDP Consent-Manager r
           +ole + GDPR + AI Act *require* what we build.                 
      1401 +5. **India-rails advantage:** DEPA/AA/DigiLocker give us popu
           +lation-scale consent infrastructure to extend, not build.    
      1402 +6. **Avoids all four failure modes** (§5.11): vertical wedge,
           + defense-in-depth (not HE-only), outcome-selling (fraud/diagn
           +ostic ROI), regulation-driven urgency.                       
      1403 +                                                             
      1404 +```mermaid                                                   
      1405 +graph TD                                                     
      1406 +    A[30 directions generated] --> B[Scored on F/I/D/P/B]    
      1407 +    B --> C[Reject: red-ocean, immature, feature-only, GTM-to
           +o-hard]                                                      
      1408 +    C --> D[Shortlist: #1,2,3,5,17,23]                       
      1409 +    D --> E[Choose #1 as platform<br/>+ #3 as wedge<br/>+ #5,
           +23 as motion<br/>+ #2,17 as expansion]                       
      1410 +    E --> F((ConsentGrid))                                   
      1411 +```

      1454 +                                                             
      1455 +## 12.2 Data-flow (training a federated model with consent)  
      1456 +                                                             
      1457 +```mermaid                                                   
      1458 +sequenceDiagram                                              
      1459 +    participant Ind as Individual (wallet)                   
      1460 +    participant CM as Consent Manager                        
      1461 +    participant Orch as Orchestrator                         
      1462 +    participant TEE as Silo Agent (TEE)                      
      1463 +    participant Agg as Secure Aggregator (TEE)               
      1464 +    participant Con as AI Builder                            
      1465 +    Con->>CM: Request collaboration (purpose, scope)         
      1466 +    CM->>Ind: Consent request (granular, time-bound)         
      1467 +    Ind->>CM: Grant (Verifiable Credential, signed)          
      1468 +    CM->>Orch: Authorized job + policy (ε budget, retention) 
      1469 +    Orch->>TEE: Dispatch model + attestation challenge       
      1470 +    TEE-->>Orch: Attestation proof (genuine enclave + code ha
           +sh)                                                          
      1471 +    TEE->>TEE: Train locally on consented records            
      1472 +    TEE->>Agg: Encrypted gradient (+ local DP noise)         
      1473 +    Agg->>Agg: Secure aggregation (no single update visible) 
      1474 +    Agg->>Con: DP-bounded global model / result              
      1475 +    Agg->>CM: Write audit entry (who/what/when/ε spent)      
      1476 +    Note over Ind,CM: Individual can later REVOKE → triggers 
           +unlearning/retrain                                           
      1477 +```                                                          
      1478 +                                                             
      1479 +## 12.3 Entity-relationship model (control plane)            
      1480 +                                                             
      1481 +```mermaid                                                   
      1482 +erDiagram                                                    
      1483 +    INDIVIDUAL ||--o{ CONSENT_GRANT : issues                 
      1484 +    INDIVIDUAL ||--|| DID : owns                             
      1485 +    CONSENT_GRANT }o--|| PURPOSE : "scoped to"               
      1486 +    CONSENT_GRANT }o--|| DATA_FIDUCIARY : "granted to"       
      1487 +    CONSENT_GRANT ||--o{ AUDIT_EVENT : generates             
      1488 +    DATA_FIDUCIARY ||--o{ SILO : operates                    
      1489 +    SILO ||--o{ DATASET : holds                              
      1490 +    COLLAB_JOB }o--o{ SILO : "runs across"                   
      1491 +    COLLAB_JOB }o--|| PURPOSE : "authorized for"             
      1492 +    COLLAB_JOB ||--o{ AUDIT_EVENT : logs                     
      1493 +    COLLAB_JOB ||--|| DP_BUDGET : "consumes"                 
      1494 +    MODEL ||--o{ COLLAB_JOB : "produced by"                  
      1495 +```                                                          
      1496 +                                                             
      1497 +## 12.4 Layer-by-layer technology choices                    
      1498 +                                                             
      1499 +| Layer | Choice | Rationale |                               
      1500 +|---|---|---|                                                
      1501 +| **Frontend** | React/Next.js (web dashboards); Flutter/Reac
           +t Native (consent wallet app) | Cross-platform; Next.js on Ve
           +rcel for speed |                                             
      1502 +| **Consent wallet** | Mobile app holding DIDs/VCs; secure en
           +clave key storage (iOS Secure Enclave / Android Keystore) | U
           +ser-held keys; selective disclosure |                        
      1503 +| **Control-plane backend** | Node.js/TypeScript or Go micros
           +ervices; OIDC/OAuth2 auth; FIDO2 | Standards-based identity |
      1504 +| **Consent/audit ledger** | Append-only log (e.g., immudb / 
           +QLDB-style) with periodic hash-anchoring; optional permission
           +ed ledger | Tamper-evidence *without* putting PII on-chain (G
           +DPR-safe) |                                                  
      1505 +| **Policy engine** | OPA/Rego-style policy-as-code | Purpose
           + limitation, retention, ε rules enforced programmatically |  
      1506 +| **FL framework** | NVIDIA FLARE / Flower / OpenFL (open-sou
           +rce) | Production-grade, avoid reinventing |                 
      1507 +| **DP** | Opacus / TensorFlow Privacy / Google DP library | 
           +Standard DP-SGD + accounting |                               
      1508 +| **TEE / confidential compute** | Azure Confidential / AWS N
           +itro / GCP Confidential Space; NVIDIA H100 CC for GPU | Multi
           +-cloud abstraction to avoid lock-in |                        
      1509 +| **SMPC/PSI** | MP-SPDZ / open PSI libs | For private joins/
           +matching |                                                   
      1510 +| **ZKP** | Groth16/Plonk libs (e.g., arkworks, circom) or Po
           +lygon ID | Selective disclosure |                            
      1511 +| **Attestation** | Intel Tiber / cloud attestation services 
           ++ open verifier | Verify enclave genuineness |               
      1512 +| **Cloud/infra** | Kubernetes; multi-cloud; silo-side agents
           + deployable on-prem | Data-residency + hybrid |              
      1513 +| **Storage** | Data stays in silos (Postgres/S3/on-prem); pl
           +atform stores only metadata + audit | No central raw-data sto
           +re — by design |                                             
      1514 +| **Observability** | OpenTelemetry + privacy-attestation das
           +hboards | Enterprise trust |                                 
      1515 +                                                             
      1516 +## 12.5 Microservices decomposition                          
      1517 +

      1519 +graph LR

      1521 +    S1[consent-service]                                      
      1522 +    S2[identity-service DID/VC]                              
      1523 +    S3[policy-engine]                                        
      1524 +    S4[orchestrator]                                         
      1525 +    S5[attestation-service]                                  
      1526 +    S6[dp-accountant]                                        
      1527 +    S7[audit-ledger]                                         
      1528 +    S8[silo-agent SDK]                                       
      1529 +    S9[model-api gateway]                                    
      1530 +    S10[billing/metering]                                    
      1531 +    end                                                      
      1532 +    S1 --> S7                                                
      1533 +    S2 --> S1                                                
      1534 +    S3 --> S4                                                
      1535 +    S4 --> S8                                                
      1536 +    S5 --> S4                                                
      1537 +    S8 --> S6

      1539 +    S4 --> S7

      1541 +```                                                          
      1542 +                                                             
      1543 +## 12.6 Scalability design                                   
      1544 +                                                             
      1545 +- **Horizontal:** stateless control-plane microservices behin
           +d a gateway; orchestrator scales per-job; silo agents scale i
           +ndependently (data-local compute).                           
      1546 +- **Cross-silo not cross-device first:** dozens–hundreds of i
           +nstitutional silos (manageable) before any billion-device amb
           +ition — matches proven FL scale (§4).                        
      1547 +- **Async & fault-tolerant:** straggler-tolerant aggregation;
           + checkpointing; job retries.                                 
      1548 +- **Multi-cloud + on-prem:** silo agent runs where the data l
           +ives (data-residency compliance is a *feature*).             
      1549 +- **Bottlenecks & mitigations:** (a) TEE memory limits → VM-l
           +evel TEEs (TDX/SEV) + partitioning; (b) FL comms → gradient c
           +ompression/secure-agg batching; (c) DP budget exhaustion → bu
           +dget scheduling + renewal governance; (d) attestation latency
           + → cache attestations per session.                           
      1550 +                                                             
      1551 +## 12.7 Security architecture                                
      1552 +                                                             
      1553 +- **Zero-trust:** every silo agent + aggregator must pass **r
           +emote attestation** before receiving models/keys.            
      1554 +- **Defense-in-depth:** FL (minimize) + TEE (isolate) + DP (b
           +ound leakage) + SMPC (private joins) — no single point of fai
           +lure.                                                        
      1555 +- **Key management:** individual holds consent keys; institut
           +ional HSM/KMS for silo keys; envelope encryption.            
      1556 +- **Threat model coverage:** honest-but-curious silos (TEE + 
           +secure-agg), malicious aggregator (attestation + DP), gradien
           +t-inversion (DP + secure-agg), model-memorization leakage (DP
           +), insider at cloud (confidential compute).                  
      1557 +- **Auditability:** every access/compute event written to tam
           +per-evident ledger; citizen-visible logs (Estonia-inspired). 
      1558 +                                                             
      1559 +---

      1579 +                                                             
      1580 +## 13.2 Cloud / operational cost drivers                     
      1581 +                                                             
      1582 +- **Confidential compute premium:** TEE VMs cost ~5–30% over 
           +standard; confidential GPUs (H100) are premium. `[APPROX]`   
      1583 +- **Egress minimized** (data stays in silos → low data-transf
           +er cost — a structural advantage).                           
      1584 +- **Cost scales with jobs, not data volume** (we don't store 
           +raw data) → attractive unit economics.                       
      1585 +                                                             
      1586 +## 13.3 Revenue models (ranked)                              
      1587 +                                                             
      1588 +| Model | How | Pros | Cons |                                
      1589 +|---|---|---|---|                                            
      1590 +| **1. Platform subscription (SaaS) per silo/seat** | Annual 
           +license per participating institution | Predictable ARR | Slo
           +w to land |                                                  
      1591 +| **2. Usage/consumption (per federated job / compute-hour)**
           + | Meter jobs, ε consumed, models trained | Scales with value
           + | Variable |                                                
      1592 +| **3. Consortium/network fee** | Orchestrate a vertical netw
           +ork (banks, hospitals), take a network fee | High value, stic
           +ky | Needs critical mass |                                   
      1593 +| **4. Consent-Manager transaction fee** | Per-consent (AA-st
           +yle micro-fee) | Population-scale volume | Thin margin, needs
           + scale |                                                     
      1594 +| **5. Compliance/attestation reporting add-on** | Premium go
           +vernance/audit module | High-margin | Add-on only |          
      1595 +                                                             
      1596 +**Recommended blend:** land with **(1) subscription** per ins
           +titution + **(2) usage** for compute, expand to **(3) network
           + fee** as verticals reach critical mass, monetize individuals
           + later via **(4)** at population scale. `[EST]`

           + Competitive | Med-High | High | Neutrality (cross-cloud, cro

      1718 +                                                             
      1719 +## 15.3 The three existential risks (and why we survive them)
      1720 +                                                             
      1721 +1. **"Nobody buys fast enough."** → Mitigated by choosing a v
           +ertical with *acute, deadline-driven* pain (DPDP + health/fin
           +ance) and selling ROI, not tech.                             
      1722 +2. **"AWS/Azure ships it free."** → They *serve institutions*
           +; they will not build the *individual-facing, cross-cloud, ne
           +utral* control plane (channel conflict + not their model). Ou
           +r moat is neutrality + the individual.                       
      1723 +3. **"The crypto breaks."** → Defense-in-depth means no singl
           +e mechanism's failure = data breach; this is the core archite
           +ctural insurance.

      1765 +# 18. MOCKUP IDEAS                                           
      1766 +                                                             
      1767 +## 18.1 Screens / dashboards                                 
      1768 +- **Individual Consent Wallet (mobile):** list of active gran
           +ts; each shows who/purpose/expiry/ε used; big **"Revoke"** bu
           +tton; access-log timeline ("Hospital X trained model Y on 202
           +7-03-04"); "Revoke & Forget" flow.                           
      1769 +- **Institution Console (web):** onboard silo; browse availab
           +le collaborations; launch federated job (pick model, purpose,
           + ε budget); attestation status; results/model registry.      
      1770 +- **DPO/Compliance Dashboard:** consent lineage; DPDP/GDPR/AI
           +-Act mapping checklist; audit export; ε-budget accounting; in
           +cident log.                                                  
      1771 +- **Regulator Oversight View (read-only):** aggregate activit
           +y, consent stats, no PII; anomaly flags.                     
      1772 +- **Developer Portal:** SDK/API docs, sandbox, "train your fi
           +rst federated model in 10 minutes."                          
      1773 +                                                             
      1774 +## 18.2 User flow (wireframe sequence)                       
      1775 +```mermaid                                                   
      1776 +graph LR                                                     
      1777 +    L1[Login / DID auth] --> L2[Dashboard]                   
      1778 +    L2 --> L3[Create collaboration]                          
      1779 +    L3 --> L4[Define purpose + scope + ε]                    
      1780 +    L4 --> L5[Consent solicitation to individuals]           
      1781 +    L5 --> L6[Attestation + launch federated job]            
      1782 +    L6 --> L7[Monitor training]                              
      1783 +    L7 --> L8[Receive DP-bounded model]                      
      1784 +    L8 --> L9[Audit report auto-generated]                   
      1785 +```                                                          
      1786 +                                                             
      1787 +## 18.3 Storyboard (demo narrative for judges)               
      1788 +1. Aarti (citizen) grants consent in her wallet, sees exactly
           + what for.                                                   
      1789 +2. Three hospitals launch a federated diabetic-retinopathy mo
           +del — no images leave.                                       
      1790 +3. Attestation turns green; training runs inside enclaves.   
      1791 +4. Model trained; DP applied; accuracy beats any single hospi
           +tal's.                                                       
      1792 +5. Aarti sees the audit entry; taps "revoke"; system schedule
           +s unlearning.                                                
      1793 +6. DPO exports a DPDP-compliant audit report in one click.

❯ can you see ppts or pdfs visually?                                            

● Partly — here's the honest breakdown:

  PDFs — yes. My Read tool renders PDF pages visually (up to 20 pages per
  request). I can see layout, images, charts, and text as they actually appear
  on the page. So if you export a deck to PDF, I can review it visually — spot
  overflow, alignment, color/contrast, cramped slides, etc.

  PPTX / PPT — no, not directly. I can't visually render a PowerPoint file. Two
  workarounds, both available on your machine since LibreOffice is installed:

  1. Convert to PDF, then I read it visually — this is the best option:
  soffice --headless --convert-to pdf yourdeck.pptx
  1. Then I Read the PDF and give you visual feedback slide-by-slide.
  2. Convert to images (PNG per slide) — I can also view individual PNGs
  directly:
  soffice --headless --convert-to pdf yourdeck.pptx
  # then pdftoppm / or export slides as PNG

  Other things I can see visually: PNG/JPG images, and I can render/inspect the
  Mermaid diagrams and mockups from the dossier if you generate them as images.

  So the practical loop for your case competition: you (or I) build the deck →
  export to PDF → I review it visually and flag design/content issues → iterate.

  Want me to set up a quick LibreOffice command that auto-converts a .pptx to
  PDF (and/or per-slide PNGs) so I can start reviewing once you have a draft
  deck? Or if you'd like, I can begin building the deck itself from the dossier
  and hand you a PDF I've already visually checked.
✻ Cogitated for 12s

❯ according to you build consulting grade winning ppts analyse which one's a    
  better tool? canva or figma by your mcp level access                          

● Direct comparison based on what each MCP toolset actually exposes to me:

  Canva MCP — built for exactly this job

  - generate-design / generate-design-structured — generates full decks from a
  prompt/content structure, including multi-page slide flow.
  - create-design-from-brand-template / search-brand-templates /
  publish-brand-template — consulting decks live and die by consistent brand
  templates (title slide, section dividers, data-slide layouts). Canva has this
  natively.
  - get-presenter-notes — speaker notes per slide, useful for the actual pitch.
  - export-design + get-export-formats — exports straight to PPTX and PDF, which
  I can then visually review (PDF) before you present.
  - resize-design — reflow to 16:9 / 4:3 / A4 handout instantly.
  - get-design-thumbnail / get-design-content — I can inspect the deck's actual
  visual state and iterate.
  - Built-in stock photos, icons, charts, and layout templates — closes the
  "make it look consulting-grade" gap without hand-building every visual asset.

  Figma MCP — built for product/UI design, not slide decks

  - get_design_context, get_screenshot, create_new_file, generate_diagram —
  excellent for mockup screens (your dossier's §18 mockups: consent wallet,
  dashboards, user flows) and for FigJam-style flowcharts/architecture diagrams.
  - No native "slide deck" or "presentation" primitive — a deck in Figma is just
  artboards on a canvas; there's no presenter-notes, no
  brand-template-for-decks, no PPTX export in the tool surface I have.
  - Its strength is pixel-perfect UI and dev-handoff (Code Connect), which is
  overkill and the wrong shape for a 15–20 slide pitch deck.

  Verdict

  Use Canva for the deck itself (this is the tool with
  generate/export/brand-template primitives built for presentations) and use 
  Figma only for the mockup screens (§18 of the dossier — consent wallet UI,
  dashboards) that you then import as images into the Canva deck.

  Concretely, my plan if you want me to proceed:
  1. Figma → generate 3–4 high-fidelity mockup screens (consent wallet,
  institution console, DPO dashboard, architecture diagram) from §12/§18 of the
  dossier.
  2. Canva → generate-design-structured a full slide deck (Problem → Solution →
  Mockups → Feasibility → Scalability) using the dossier as source content,
  embedding the Figma-exported mockup images and the Mermaid-derived charts.
  3. Export to PDF, and I visually review it slide-by-slide before you present.

  Want me to start with step 1 (Figma mockups) or jump straight to step 2 (Canva
  deck) using placeholder mockup visuals for now?

❯ will figma be easier?                                                         

● Possibly, yes — let me check the actual Figma tool constraints before
  answering, since my Canva assessment was based on documentation, not hands-on
  friction like we just hit.

● Yes — based on what I just found, Figma is genuinely easier for this specific
  job, and here's the concrete evidence, not just theory:

  The blocker I hit with Canva: its asset-upload tool only accepts
  already-public HTTPS URLs — my 15 locally-generated charts/mockups can't go in
  without either a public host (which the tool explicitly warns against
  defaulting to) or me manually driving a browser upload. Its editing API is
  also constrained to text/image swaps on existing elements — no native "draw a
  chevron ribbon" or "lay out 5 panels precisely," which is exactly what
  EY-density requires.

  What Figma actually offers:
  - upload_assets takes raw image bytes directly (POST to a returned upload URL)
  — no public hosting needed at all. My 15 PNGs go straight in.
  - use_figma runs arbitrary code against the real Figma Plugin API — full
  pixel-level control over exact panel grids, ribbons, chips, badges, spacing.
  This is a categorically better fit for "dense EY-style multi-panel layout, not
  a generic AI template."
  - Figma Slides (editorType: "slides") is a native presentation file type — so
  I get a real deck container, not just a design canvas.
  - No outline-approval-widget gate blocking programmatic generation.

  The tradeoff: Figma won't auto-draft slide copy the way Canva's AI outline did
  — I write every panel/element explicitly via code, which is more calls from
  me but means zero "vibecoded" filler, exactly what you asked for. Final export
  to PDF/PPTX is one extra step (already have a working LibreOffice/PyMuPDF
  pipeline from this session).

  Recommendation: switch to Figma Slides for the build. Want me to proceed? I'll
  need to call whoami first to get your team/plan key, then create the file and
  start building the 11 slides using the 15 assets already generated.

     │ card underneath.                                                        │
     │ 2. Layout variety is the point: benchmark decks do NOT repeat one       │
     │ template. Rails/bands appear only on the densest slides; other slides   │

     PLAN v3 — Typography Amplification + Content Densification Pass

     Context

     The v2 rebuild (layered cards, supply-chain ribbon, native-vector
     everything, per-slide architecture) landed extremely well — user called it
     "phenomenal." This is a refinement pass on the same 11-slide deck
     (2uKgT9OUWIhGR3qjn4IsnG), not a rebuild. Two concrete problems remain, per
     direct user feedback:

     1. Typography is still too small in many places — several text roles (panel
     body text, chart/axis labels, mockup UI text, some KPI numerals) sit below
     what reads comfortably from a distance, and a few KPI numerals (Slide 6
     cost cards at 34pt, Slide 8 dashboard at 28pt) never actually hit the
     56–72pt spec from v2 — they were missed during build. The user explicitly
     wants mockup/diagram/chart text bumped too, overriding my earlier judgment
     call to keep mockup UI text small for "realism."
     2. Several slides still read as under-filled — real whitespace gaps exist
     (Slide 8 between timeline and KPI row; Appendix middle band; Slide 4 lower
     third; Slide 3 below failure cards) that should be closed with more real 
     content pulled from research-dossier.md, not just decoration.

     This is a surgical patch pass: bump font sizes in place where geometry
     allows, enlarge/rebuild only the specific regions that need more room
     (mockups, a few KPI cards), and add new content blocks only into identified
     empty zones — not a full slide demolition.

     A. Typography v3 — corrected minimums (replaces v2 table where stricter)

     ┌───────────────────────────┬─────────────────┬──────────────────────────┐
     │           Role            │   v2 actual     │        v3 target         │
     │                           │     (found)     │                          │

     │                           │                 │ dashboard KPIs (28→60)   │

     Rule: no content text below 14pt anywhere, no exceptions (previous "mockup
     realism" carve-out is retracted per explicit user instruction).

     B. Per-slide font-size patch (in-place edits, no rebuild)

     For each slide, find text nodes via findAllWithCriteria({types:["TEXT"]}),
     load each node's current font via getStyledTextSegments(['fontName']), then
     bump fontSize per the table above. Where a parent card/panel is now too
     short for the larger text, resize the parent frame first (frames don't
     auto-grow), keeping the layered back-card in sync (+offset).

     - S1 Paradox: 5-Whys body 14.5→17; harm matrix stakeholder names 16→18,
     score chips 18→20.
     - S2 Market: CAGR bar names 13→16, CAGR pill 12→15, bar value 16→18;
     TAM/SAM/SOM inner labels 12–15→16–20 (may require enlarging SOM's inner
     circle slightly, see D); timeline year 14→16, timeline sub-label 12→15; AA
     card description 15→17.
     - S3 Whitespace: fix-item body 15→17; failure-card description 14→16;
     quadrant dot labels 12→15 (widen label boxes accordingly).
     - S4 Solution: plane descriptors 14→17; why-wins body 14.5→17; ring labels
     12–15→16–19 (SMPC innermost label needs the ring enlarged slightly);
     mapping row text 13–14→16.
     - S6 Feasibility: proof-card label 15→17, citation 13→15; TRL row names
     13→16, TRL badge 11→14; cost/time KPI numerals 34→64 (bring in line with
     spec — resize those two KPI cards taller to fit); risk/mitigation text
     13/12→16/15.
     - S7 Model: KPI numerals 44→64 (resize KPI cards taller); ARR value pills
     13→16, year labels 11→14; tornado % labels 11→14, variable names 12→15;
     revenue chips 12.5→15.
     - S8 Scale: timeline phase title 14→17, sub-label 12→15, node number 22→26;
     KPI dashboard numerals 28→60 (resize those 4 cards taller — currently the
     clear outlier); Ask body 19→22.
     - Appendix: table body 11.5→15, headers 13→16; scenario card body 11.5→15;
     glossary chip text 11.5→15.
     - Cover / Thank You: already compliant, no changes.

     C. Content densification (new blocks, sourced from research-dossier.md, 
     placed only in confirmed empty zones)

     - S3 Whitespace (gap below the 3 failure cards, ~y 810–1000): add a callout
     strip — "$450M+ raised then wound down" across TripleBlind + Hazy (dossier
     §5.11 failed-startup lessons), styled as a layered stat card.
     - S4 Solution (gap in lower third after mapping row, ~y 970–1060, tighten
     if needed): add a "Built on proven standards" chip row — NVIDIA FLARE ·
     OpenFL · W3C DID/VC · Flower (dossier §22.4 open-source/standards list).
     - S8 Scale (gap between timeline and KPI/Ask row, ~y 440–590): add a "Why 
     India First" 3-bullet horizontal strip pulling from dossier §2.4 — DPDP
     regulatory clock, DEPA/Account-Aggregator rails already live, India
     cost-base advantage.
     - Appendix (dead band between the two top tables and the bottom row, ~y
     430–700): add a "Selected Competitor Landscape" mini-table (5–6 rows:
     Owkin, Duality, OneTrust, Snowflake Clean Rooms, Zama, Account Aggregator —
     funding/model/moat columns condensed from dossier §5), full width,
     EY-style bordered.
     - S2 Market: add one more stat beneath the CAGR bars — Gartner PET-adoption
     figure (dossier §2.6 / §19 stat #22: "60% of large orgs to use ≥1 PET by
     2025") as a small stat strip, closing the visual gap under the bar chart.

     No new slides are added — all 11 remain; content grows within existing
     slides only, per user's direction.

     D. Slide 5 Product — mockup enlargement (the one true rebuild-in-place)

     Mockup UI text jumping from 9–11pt to 13–15pt won't fit the current frame
     sizes without reflow. Rebuild just the 3 mockup sub-trees (not the whole
     slide):
     - Tighten column gap (32→20px) to reclaim width; increase each mockup frame
     ~15–20% (phone 260×490 → ~300×560; browser/dashboard panels similarly
     enlarged to fill the freed space).
     - Re-lay internal rows with the new text sizes and proportionally taller
     row heights (e.g., grant rows 62→72px, attestation rows spaced 20→26px).
     - Keep exactly the same information (no cuts) — just larger and roomier.
     - Flow strip below: bump label 12→14, keep punch-hole/wire motif unchanged
     (already reads well at distance).

     E-bis. EY late-slide density benchmark (Business Financials, Appendix — 
     pages 7, 9, 11–12)

     Re-studied these EY pages specifically per user request. Patterns present
     there that our deck still under-uses:
     - Charts carry bold embedded annotations, not just external KPI cards —
     e.g. EY's break-even line chart has a dashed vertical marker with a bold
     in-chart callout ("Breakeven on CM level: 3 yrs") sitting directly on the
     plot, not off to the side.
     - Real tabular data, not only cards/bars — EY's financials slide includes
     an actual small multi-column table (Year × Revenue × Variable Cost ×
     Contribution Margin) alongside the charts, not instead of them.
     - Multiple distinct chart types combined on one slide — EY runs a line
     chart + a stacked bar chart + two donut charts together with bullet
     commentary, all on a single Business Financials slide. Density comes from
     chart-type variety, not repetition.
     - Rail-labeled commentary panels ("Opportunity for EY") sit beside a chart
     and narrate what it means in 3–4 bullets — same rail pattern we already
     use, just also applied as chart commentary, not only as section dividers.
     - Appendix as a genuine reference wall — EY's appendix pages are dense,
     varied reference material (a labeled process diagram, a regulation bullet
     list, a two-column news-clipping wall with source logo + headline + summary
     + date). Confirms our Appendix should keep growing with real varied
     blocks, not just tables.

     Fold-ins to sections B/C above:
     - S7 Model gets denser, EY-style: add a compact 3-row financial summary
     table (Year 1–3 → Revenue, Opex, Contribution Margin, from dossier §13)
     beneath/beside the KPI row; annotate the ARR chart directly with a bold
     "Break-even ≈ Year 3" callout at the dashed line (embedded on-chart, not
     just the existing off-chart label); add a small revenue-mix donut chart
     (Subscription vs. Usage vs. Network-fee split) using freed space, with a
     short bullet commentary panel next to it explaining the mix rationale
     (rail-labeled, matching our existing band style).
     - Appendix: in addition to the new Selected-Competitor table (section C),
     treat the appendix as an EY-style reference wall — the added table should
     carry a short bullet commentary line underneath explaining "why these six,"
     consistent with EY pairing tables with narrative bullets rather than
     leaving tables to speak alone.

     E. Execution order

     1. Font-size patch pass, slide-by-slide (S1→S8, Appendix), using in-place
     text mutation; resize parent cards where the new text needs more room;
     re-run the overlap/out-of-bounds validation script after each slide.
     2. Content-addition pass: S3, S4, S8, Appendix, S2 — add the five new
     blocks described in C, each followed by validation.
     3. S7 EY-density enhancement (E-bis) — add the 3-row financial table,
     on-chart break-even annotation, revenue-mix donut + commentary panel;
     re-validate (this slide will need careful re-layout since it's already full
     — likely trim the tornado chart's footprint slightly or extend vertically
     into currently unused margin).
     4. Appendix commentary line under the new competitor table (E-bis).
     5. Slide 5 mockup rebuild (D) — remove only the 3 mockup sub-trees, rebuild
     larger with bigger text, validate.
     6. Final full-deck sweep: assert zero text node <14pt anywhere (down from
     the v2 sweep's tolerance for mockup text), zero overlaps, zero
     out-of-bounds, confirm every "hero stat" KPI card is now 60–76pt.
     7. Screenshot all 11 slides fresh, present to user.

     Verification

  Biggest Strengths                                                             
                                                                                
  Visually, this is one of the strongest undergraduate decks I've seen          
  recently. The consistent purple-yellow design language, modular layouts, and  
  page flow feel polished. The storyline progresses logically from problem to   
  market to solution to feasibility to business model and scale.                
                                                                                
  The product concept is genuinely differentiated. Most teams propose "privacy  
  platform"; you've articulated an orchestration layer around consent, compute, 
  and privacy-preserving AI.                                                    
                                                                                
  The appendix is also unusually strong compared to typical student             
  submissions.                                                                  
                                                                                
  Biggest Weaknesses                                                            
                                                                                
  These are the issues that would stop me from giving this first place.         
                                                                                
  1. No proof customers actually want this                                      
                                                                                
  You explain the technology.                                                   
                                                                                
  You explain regulation.                                                       
                                                                                
  You never prove demand.                                                       
                                                                                
  Where are:                                                                    
                                                                                
  interviews                                                                    
  hospital CIO quotes                                                           
  DPO interviews                                                                
  bank interviews                                                               
  willingness-to-pay                                                            
  pilot LOIs                                                                    
                                                                                
  Without these, judges think:                                                  
                                                                                
  "Great technology. Unknown customer demand."                                  
                                                                                
  2. Market sizing isn't defendable                                             
                                                                                
  You state:                                                                    
                                                                                
  TAM                                                                           
  SAM                                                                           
  SOM                                                                           
                                                                                
  But you never show:                                                           
                                                                                
  "How did you calculate SOM?"                                                  
                                                                                
  McKinsey judges almost always ask this.                                       
                                                                                
  3. Financial model is too high level                                          
                                                                                
  A consulting judge immediately asks:                                          
                                                                                
  Why exactly is ACV $300k?                                                     
                                                                                
  Why CAC $150k?                                                                
                                                                                
  Why 70% margins?                                                              
                                                                                
  Why break-even at 30 customers?                                               
                                                                                
  None of those assumptions are justified.                                      
                                                                                
  4. GTM is weak                                                                
                                                                                
  This is probably the largest strategic weakness.                              
                                                                                
  You don't explain:                                                            
                                                                                
  Who buys?                                                                     
                                                                                
  Why first?                                                                    
                                                                                
  Sales cycle?                                                                  
                                                                                
  Decision maker?                                                               
                                                                                
  Procurement?                                                                  
                                                                                
  Pilot conversion?                                                             
                                                                                
  Expansion strategy?                                                           
                                                                                
  5. No ROI                                                                     
                                                                                
  Every enterprise software judge asks:                                         
                                                                                
  "If I buy this tomorrow...                                                    
                                                                                
  how much money do I save?"                                                    
                                                                                
  That answer is missing.                                                       
                                                                                
  6. Customer journey missing                                                   
                                                                                
  There is no slide showing:                                                    
                                                                                
  Current workflow                                                              
                                                                                
  ↓                                                                             
                                                                                
  ConsentGrid                                                                   
                                                                                
  ↓                                                                             
                                                                                
  Future workflow                                                               
                                                                                
  That usually appears in winning decks.                                        
                                                                                
  7. Missing implementation roadmap                                             
                                                                                
  The roadmap is strategic.                                                     
                                                                                
  It isn't operational.                                                         
                                                                                
  Need:                                                                         
                                                                                
  Month 1                                                                       
                                                                                
  Month 3                                                                       
                                                                                
  Month 6                                                                       
                                                                                
  Month 9                                                                       
                                                                                
  Pilot                                                                         
                                                                                
  Security review                                                               
                                                                                
  Deployment                                                                    
                                                                                
  Expansion                                                                     
                                                                                
  8. Competitive positioning is incomplete                                      
                                                                                
  Current competitor map is interesting.                                        
                                                                                
  But it isn't enough.                                                          
                                                                                
  Judges want:                                                                  
                                                                                
  Feature matrix                                                                
                                                                                
  Capability comparison                                                         
                                                                                
  Pricing comparison                                                            
                                                                                
  Technology comparison                                                         
                                                                                
  Switching costs                                                               
                                                                                
  Moats                                                                         
                                                                                
  9. Technology dominates business                                              
                                                                                
  About 60% of the presentation explains technology.                            
                                                                                
  Only about 25% explains business.                                             
                                                                                
  Winning consulting decks are usually the reverse.                             
                                                                                
  10. Investment thesis isn't fully developed                                   
                                                                                
  An investor wants:                                                            
                                                                                
  Why now?                                                                      
                                                                                
  Why us?                                                                       
                                                                                
  Why impossible to copy?                                                       
                                                                                
  Why huge?                                                                     
                                                                                
  You answer these partially.                                                   
                                                                                
  Not completely.
